<a id='question-130'></a>

### Question 130: The Immune System: Part 3 Focus 30 (killer)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and Natural Killer (NK) cells in cell-mediated immunity, specifically their mechanisms of target cell killing and the ligands involved (e.g., FasL, perforin/granzymes). Distinguishing between CTL and NK cell activation and target recognition. (理解细胞毒性T淋巴细胞(CTL)和自然杀伤(NK)细胞在细胞介导免疫中的作用，特别是它们的目标细胞杀伤机制和涉及的配体(例如，FasL、穿孔素/颗粒酶)。区分CTL和NK细胞的激活和目标识别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with a history of chronic lymphocytic leukemia (CLL) undergoes an allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) from an HLA-matched unrelated donor. The conditioning regimen includes fludarabine and cyclophosphamide. Post-transplant, the patient develops severe graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) affecting the skin, liver, and gastrointestinal tract. Despite immunosuppressive therapy with cyclosporine and methotrexate, the GVHD progresses. Biopsies of the affected skin show extensive apoptosis of keratinocytes and endothelial cells, with infiltration by donor-derived lymphocytes. Flow cytometry analysis of the donor lymphocytes reveals a significant population of CD8+ T cells expressing high levels of Fas ligand (FasL) and granzyme B. Further investigation shows that the patient's recipient cells express high levels of Fas receptor (Fas). Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed tissue damage in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by donor NK cells recognizing recipient cells coated with anti-Fas antibodies.
(B) Perforin/granzyme-mediated cytotoxicity by donor CD8+ T cells recognizing recipient cells presenting donor MHC class I molecules.
(C) FasL/Fas-mediated apoptosis induced by donor CD8+ T cells interacting with recipient cells expressing Fas.
(D) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by donor antibodies binding to Fas receptor on recipient cells.
(E) Phagocytosis of recipient cells by donor macrophages expressing Fc receptors specific for anti-Fas antibodies.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male with CLL undergoing allogeneic HSCT. This sets the stage for potential GVHD.
- **Conditioning Regimen:** Fludarabine and cyclophosphamide are immunosuppressive agents used before HSCT to deplete the recipient's immune system and prevent rejection of the graft.
- **Post-Transplant Complication:** Severe GVHD (skin, liver, GI tract) despite immunosuppression. GVHD occurs when donor T cells recognize the recipient's tissues as foreign and attack them.
- **Histopathology:** Apoptosis of keratinocytes and endothelial cells in skin biopsies, with donor lymphocyte infiltration. Apoptosis is a key feature of GVHD-mediated tissue damage.
- **Immunophenotyping:** Donor lymphocytes are CD8+ T cells (CTLs) expressing high levels of FasL and granzyme B. This points towards CTL-mediated cytotoxicity.
- **Recipient Cell Expression:** Recipient cells express high levels of Fas receptor. Fas is a death receptor that, when bound by FasL, triggers apoptosis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism of tissue damage.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD):** A major complication of allogeneic HSCT where donor T cells (primarily CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ CTLs) recognize recipient tissues as foreign due to MHC differences (alloantigens).
- **CD8+ T Cells (CTLs):** Key mediators of cell-mediated immunity. They recognize peptide antigens presented on MHC class I molecules on target cells. Upon activation, CTLs kill target cells through two main pathways:
    - **Granule Exocytosis Pathway:** CTLs release cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes. Perforin forms pores in the target cell membrane, allowing granzymes (serine proteases) to enter and activate caspases, leading to apoptosis.
    - **FasL/Fas Pathway:** CTLs express Fas ligand (FasL, also known as CD95L) on their surface. FasL binds to the Fas receptor (also known as CD95 or APO-1) on target cells. This interaction triggers the formation of the death-inducing signaling complex (DISC) within the target cell, activating caspases and inducing apoptosis.
- **Fas/FasL System:** A crucial pathway for inducing apoptosis in various physiological and pathological processes, including immune regulation, development, and elimination of infected or cancerous cells. In GVHD, CTLs utilize FasL to directly induce apoptosis in recipient cells expressing Fas.
- **NK Cells:** Also involved in cytotoxicity, but they recognize target cells differently (e.g., missing self MHC class I, stress ligands). While NK cells can contribute to GVHD, the vignette specifically highlights CD8+ T cells expressing FasL and granzymes, and recipient cells expressing Fas, pointing towards the FasL/Fas pathway as a major mechanism.
- **ADCC:** Involves antibodies coating target cells, which are then recognized by Fc receptors on effector cells (like NK cells or macrophages). This is not the primary mechanism described here, as the key interaction is FasL/Fas.
- **CDC:** Involves antibodies binding to target cells, followed by complement activation and formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC), leading to cell lysis. This is not the mechanism described.
- **Phagocytosis:** Involves effector cells (like macrophages) engulfing and destroying target cells. This is not the primary mechanism described.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. ADCC requires antibodies coating the target cells and effector cells with Fc receptors. The vignette describes a direct cell-cell interaction via FasL/Fas, not antibody-mediated recognition. While NK cells can mediate ADCC, the primary cytotoxic mechanism highlighted in the vignette involves CD8+ T cells and FasL/Fas interaction.
- (B): Incorrect. Perforin/granzyme-mediated cytotoxicity is indeed a mechanism used by CD8+ T cells. However, the vignette specifically mentions high expression of FasL on donor CD8+ T cells and high expression of Fas on recipient cells, making the FasL/Fas pathway a more directly implicated mechanism based on the provided data. While both pathways can occur, the FasL/Fas interaction is explicitly highlighted.
- (D): Incorrect. CDC requires antibodies and complement activation. The vignette describes a direct cell-cell interaction mediated by FasL/Fas, not complement.
- (E): Incorrect. Phagocytosis involves macrophages engulfing target cells. The vignette describes apoptosis induced by CTLs, not phagocytosis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Correct Answer (C) - FasL/Fas Pathway | Distractor (A) - ADCC | Distractor (B) - Perforin/Granzyme | Distractor (D) - CDC | Distractor (E) - Phagocytosis |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------|----------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------|------------------------------|
| **Mediator**         | CD8+ T cells (FasL)                   | NK cells (FcR)       | CD8+ T cells (Granules)           | Antibodies (IgG/IgM) | Macrophages (FcR)            |
| **Target Receptor**  | Fas (on recipient cells)              | FcR (on effector)    | MHC I (on target)                 | Target cell surface | FcR (on phagocyte)            |
| **Mechanism**        | Apoptosis via DISC activation         | Antibody-coated cell lysis | Apoptosis via caspase activation | Cell lysis via MAC   | Engulfment & degradation     |
| **Key Molecules**    | FasL, Fas                              | Antibody, FcR        | Perforin, Granzymes               | Complement proteins | FcR                          |
| **Relevance to GVHD**| Major CTL-mediated pathway            | Can occur, less emphasized | Major CTL-mediated pathway        | Not primary          | Not primary                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary mechanism of CTL-mediated cytotoxicity involves either the perforin/granzyme pathway or the FasL/Fas pathway, both leading to apoptosis of the target cell. In the context of GVHD, both pathways contribute, but the specific mention of high FasL expression on donor CTLs and high Fas expression on recipient cells strongly implicates the FasL/Fas pathway as a key driver of tissue damage. Differentiate this from NK cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC, missing self), antibody-mediated mechanisms (ADCC, CDC), and phagocytosis. Remember that CTLs are crucial effectors in GVHD, targeting recipient alloantigens presented on MHC class I.

<a id='question-131'></a>

### Question 131: Immunodeficiency & Opportunistic Infection
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of specific immunodeficiencies, particularly combined immunodeficiencies affecting both T and B cell function, and their association with specific opportunistic infections. It requires differentiating between various primary immunodeficiencies based on clinical presentation and immunological findings. (本题考察特定免疫缺陷，特别是影响T细胞和B细胞功能的联合免疫缺陷，以及它们与特定机会性感染的相关性。需要根据临床表现和免疫学发现区分各种原发性免疫缺陷。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections (otitis media, pneumonia, sinusitis) starting at 6 months of age. He has also experienced chronic diarrhea and failure to thrive. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. Immunological workup shows markedly reduced T cell counts (CD3+ < 100/µL), absent T cell proliferation in response to mitogens, and severely decreased levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM). Flow cytometry reveals absent CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, but normal B cell numbers (CD19+). Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *RAG1* gene. Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying immunological defect?

(A) Defective B cell maturation due to impaired class switching
(B) Impaired T cell receptor (TCR) gene rearrangement
(C) Deficient complement component C3
(D) Defective phagocyte chemotaxis
(E) Absent CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections, chronic diarrhea, failure to thrive:** These symptoms in a young child strongly suggest an underlying immunodeficiency, particularly affecting both humoral (antibody-mediated) and cell-mediated immunity, as these infections are often caused by encapsulated bacteria, viruses, fungi, and protozoa.
- **Onset at 6 months:** This timing is typical for primary immunodeficiencies involving T cells, as maternal antibodies wane around this age, leaving the infant vulnerable.
- **Lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly:** Can be seen in various immunodeficiencies, sometimes due to chronic infections or autoimmune phenomena.
- **Markedly reduced T cell counts (CD3+ < 100/µL), absent T cell proliferation:** This points directly to a severe T cell defect.
- **Severely decreased IgG, IgA, IgM:** Indicates a profound humoral immunodeficiency. Since T cells are crucial for B cell activation and antibody production (T-dependent responses), the severe T cell defect explains the hypogammaglobulinemia.
- **Normal B cell numbers (CD19+):** This is a crucial clue. It indicates that B cells are present but are likely non-functional due to lack of T cell help.
- ***RAG1* gene mutation:** This is the definitive diagnostic clue. *RAG1* and *RAG2* genes encode recombination-activating genes essential for V(D)J recombination, the process by which T cell receptors (TCRs) and B cell receptors (BCRs) are generated.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **V(D)J Recombination:** This process is fundamental for adaptive immunity. It involves somatic recombination of gene segments (Variable, Diversity, Joining) to create unique antigen receptors (TCRs and BCRs) on lymphocytes. The RAG1/RAG2 complex is the enzyme responsible for initiating these double-strand breaks in DNA, allowing the segments to be joined.
- **TCR and BCR Generation:** Both T cells and B cells undergo V(D)J recombination. T cells require functional TCRs for antigen recognition and signaling, while B cells require functional BCRs (surface immunoglobulin) for antigen recognition and signaling.
- **Consequences of *RAG1* Mutation:** A mutation in *RAG1* leads to a complete block in V(D)J recombination. This results in the absence of functional TCRs on T cells and functional BCRs on B cells.
- **Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID):** This condition is characterized by profound defects in both T cell and B cell function. The clinical presentation described (early onset severe infections, failure to thrive, hypogammaglobulinemia, lymphopenia) is classic for SCID.
- **Specific Type of SCID:** The *RAG1* mutation causes a specific type of SCID often referred to as RAG deficiency SCID. It results in absent T cells and non-functional B cells (although B cells may be present in normal numbers, they cannot mature properly or produce antibodies). This contrasts with other forms of SCID, such as X-linked SCID (due to CD40L deficiency) or ADA deficiency, which have different underlying mechanisms.
- **CD40L Deficiency (X-linked SCID):** In this condition, T cells lack CD40L, which is necessary to bind to CD40 on B cells, antigen-presenting cells (APCs), and other cells. This impairs T cell help for B cell activation and antibody production, as well as T cell interactions with APCs. However, T cells themselves are usually present (though functionally impaired in cell-mediated immunity), and B cells are present.
- **T cell proliferation:** The absence of T cell proliferation in response to mitogens (like PHA or ConA) indicates a fundamental defect in T cell function, consistent with the lack of functional TCRs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective B cell maturation due to impaired class switching:** This describes Hyper-IgM syndrome, often caused by mutations in *CD40LG* (CD40L deficiency) or *AID* (activation-induced cytidine deaminase). While it leads to low IgG, IgA, and IgE, IgM levels are typically normal or high. T cell numbers and function are usually normal (except in CD40L deficiency, which is a form of SCID). The vignette describes absent T cells and low levels of *all* immunoglobulin isotypes, making this incorrect.
- (C) **Deficient complement component C3:** C3 deficiency leads to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (like *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Haemophilus influenzae*) due to impaired opsonization and phagocytosis. However, it does not typically cause severe T cell lymphopenia or profound defects in cell-mediated immunity. T cell counts and function would be normal.
- (D) **Defective phagocyte chemotaxis:** This is characteristic of Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (LAD), often due to defects in integrins (e.g., CD18). Patients present with recurrent bacterial infections, impaired wound healing, and delayed umbilical cord separation. Lymphocyte numbers and function are typically normal.
- (E) **Absent CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells:** This causes X-linked SCID. T cells lack CD40L, preventing proper interaction with CD40 on B cells (impairing T-dependent antibody production) and APCs. While this leads to severe B cell dysfunction and hypogammaglobulinemia, T cells are usually present (though functionally impaired in cell-mediated immunity) and can proliferate in response to mitogens. The vignette specifically states *absent* T cell proliferation and *markedly reduced* T cell counts, which is more characteristic of a defect in T cell development itself (like RAG deficiency) rather than just impaired T cell help. Furthermore, the *RAG1* mutation points directly to a defect in TCR/BCR generation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | RAG1 Deficiency (SCID) | CD40L Deficiency (X-linked SCID) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome (CD40/AID) | C3 Deficiency | LAD |
|-------------------------|------------------------|---------------------------------|-------------------------------|---------------|-----|
| **T Cell Count**        | Absent/Markedly Low    | Present (Functionally Impaired) | Normal                        | Normal        | Normal |
| **T Cell Proliferation**| Absent                 | Present                          | Normal                        | Normal        | Normal |
| **B Cell Count**        | Normal/Low             | Normal                           | Normal                        | Normal        | Normal |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | All Low                | All Low (except IgM often normal) | Low IgG, IgA, IgE; Normal/High IgM | Normal        | Normal |
| **Primary Defect**      | V(D)J Recombination    | T Cell Help to B Cells           | Class Switch/B Cell Activation | Complement    | Phagocyte Adhesion |
| **Gene Involved**       | *RAG1*                 | *CD40LG*                         | *CD40*, *AID*                  | *C3*          | *ITGB2* (CD18) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The vignette describes a classic presentation of Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) with absent T cells and non-functional B cells, leading to severe infections early in life. The *RAG1* mutation specifically points to a defect in V(D)J recombination, which is essential for the development of both T cell receptors (TCRs) and B cell receptors (BCRs). This distinguishes it from other forms of SCID like CD40L deficiency (X-linked SCID), where T cells are present but cannot help B cells, or Hyper-IgM syndromes, where class switching is impaired. Remember that absent T cells and absent T cell proliferation are hallmarks of defects in T cell development itself.

<a id='question-132'></a>

### Question 132: The Immune System: Part 3 Focus 32 (innate)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement activation pathways (classical, alternative, lectin) and their regulation, specifically focusing on the deficiency of a late complement component leading to susceptibility to specific encapsulated bacteria. (理解补体激活途径（经典、替代、凝集素）及其调控的作用，特别是晚期补体成分缺乏导致对特定荚膜细菌易感性的机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 2-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with recurrent episodes of severe bacterial infections, including pneumonia, meningitis, and sepsis. His infections are primarily caused by encapsulated bacteria, notably *Neisseria meningitidis* and *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. He has a history of recurrent otitis media and sinusitis since infancy. Family history reveals that his maternal grandfather also suffered from recurrent infections with similar organisms. Laboratory investigations show normal levels of early complement components (C1q, C4, C3) but significantly reduced levels of terminal complement components (C5b-9, also known as the membrane attack complex or MAC). Flow cytometry analysis of his neutrophils reveals normal expression of CD16 (FcγRIII) and CD32 (FcγRII). Given this clinical presentation and laboratory findings, which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect?

(A) Deficiency in C3 convertase leading to impaired opsonization.
(B) Deficiency in C5 convertase leading to impaired bacterial lysis.
(C) Deficiency in the mannose-binding lectin (MBL) pathway leading to impaired complement activation.
(D) Deficiency in the alternative pathway properdin leading to impaired complement activation.
(E) Deficiency in the classical pathway C1 complex leading to impaired immune complex clearance.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial infections:** Suggests an underlying immunodeficiency.
- **Encapsulated bacteria (*N. meningitidis*, *S. pneumoniae*):** These organisms are particularly susceptible to complement-mediated lysis via the MAC. This points towards a defect in the terminal complement pathway.
- **Age of onset (2 years old):** Consistent with an inherited immunodeficiency.
- **Family history (maternal grandfather):** Suggests a possible X-linked or autosomal recessive inheritance pattern.
- **Normal early complement components (C1q, C4, C3):** Rules out deficiencies in the classical pathway (C1q, C4, C2) or the alternative pathway (C3, Factor B, Factor D, Properdin) *before* C5 activation. It also rules out C3 deficiency, which would affect all pathways.
- **Reduced terminal complement components (C5b-9/MAC):** This is the most critical finding, directly indicating a defect in the formation or function of the membrane attack complex.
- **Normal CD16 and CD32 on neutrophils:** Rules out defects in Fcγ receptors, which are involved in opsonization and phagocytosis, but not directly in MAC formation.

These clues collectively point to a deficiency in the terminal complement pathway, specifically affecting the formation of C5b-9. C5 convertase (C5b6789) cleaves C5 into C5a and C5b. C5b initiates the assembly of the MAC (C5b-9). A deficiency in C5 convertase would impair the generation of C5b, thus preventing MAC formation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is a crucial part of the innate immune system. It consists of over 30 proteins that act in a cascade to opsonize pathogens, recruit inflammatory cells, and directly lyse pathogens via the MAC.
- **Complement Pathways:**
    - **Classical Pathway:** Activated by antibody-antigen complexes (IgG, IgM) or directly by certain pathogens. Involves C1q, C4, C2, C3.
    - **Alternative Pathway:** Activated spontaneously on pathogen surfaces or by certain microbial components (e.g., LPS). Involves C3, Factor B, Factor D, Properdin.
    - **Lectin Pathway:** Activated by mannose-binding lectin (MBL) or ficolins binding to microbial carbohydrates. Involves MBL/ficolins, MASPs, C4, C2, C3.
- **Convergence:** All three pathways converge at the activation of C3 convertase (C4b2a for classical/lectin, C3bBb for alternative), which cleaves C3 into C3a and C3b. C3b is a potent opsonin. C3 convertase is further processed to form C5 convertase (C4b2a3b for classical/lectin, C3bBb3b for alternative).
- **Terminal Pathway:** C5 convertase cleaves C5 into C5a (anaphylatoxin) and C5b. C5b initiates the assembly of the MAC (C5b-9) on the pathogen surface, leading to lysis.
- **C5 Convertase Deficiency:** A deficiency in C5 convertase (either C5b6789 or the enzymes that generate it, like C5b) prevents the cleavage of C5 and subsequent formation of the MAC. This specifically impairs the ability to lyse encapsulated bacteria like *Neisseria* species, which are particularly susceptible to MAC-mediated killing.
- **Clinical Manifestations of Terminal Complement Deficiency:** Patients typically present with recurrent infections by *Neisseria* species (especially *N. meningitidis*), leading to meningococcal disease. They are also susceptible to other encapsulated bacteria. Autoimmune diseases like systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) are less common because C5b-9 helps clear immune complexes.

##### 3. Pathophysiology of *Neisseria* Susceptibility to MAC:
- *Neisseria* species lack a cell wall, making them inherently susceptible to lysis.
- The outer membrane of *Neisseria* is relatively permeable, allowing the MAC to insert and form pores, leading to cell death.
- Encapsulation does not protect *Neisseria* from MAC-mediated lysis; in fact, it may facilitate MAC assembly.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deficiency in C3 convertase leading to impaired opsonization.
    - **Incorrect.** C3 convertase deficiency (e.g., Factor B deficiency in the alternative pathway, C2 deficiency in the classical/lectin pathway) would impair C3b production, reducing opsonization and phagocytosis. While this causes immunodeficiency, it typically presents with a broader range of infections (pyogenic bacteria, encapsulated bacteria) and doesn't specifically explain the normal C3 levels and the specific susceptibility to *Neisseria* seen here. The vignette states normal C3 levels, which argues against a deficiency *upstream* of C3 activation, although C3 convertase acts *on* C3. However, the *terminal* deficiency is much more specific for *Neisseria* susceptibility.
- (C) Deficiency in the mannose-binding lectin (MBL) pathway leading to impaired complement activation.
    - **Incorrect.** MBL deficiency impairs the lectin pathway, which is one of the three activation pathways. While it can lead to increased susceptibility to infections, particularly encapsulated bacteria, it doesn't specifically explain the normal early complement components (C4, C3) and the *isolated* deficiency of terminal components (C5b-9). MBL deficiency would typically affect C4 and C3 levels to some extent, depending on the degree of deficiency and the compensatory activation by other pathways.
- (D) Deficiency in the alternative pathway properdin leading to impaired complement activation.
    - **Incorrect.** Properdin deficiency impairs the alternative pathway, leading to reduced C3b deposition and opsonization, particularly on bacterial surfaces. This results in increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, similar to C3 convertase deficiency. However, like option A and C, it doesn't specifically explain the normal C3 levels and the isolated deficiency of terminal components (C5b-9). Properdin deficiency would impair the *initial* steps of the alternative pathway, not the terminal pathway itself.
- (E) Deficiency in the classical pathway C1 complex leading to impaired immune complex clearance.
    - **Incorrect.** C1q, C1r, C1s deficiency impairs the classical pathway activation, primarily affecting the clearance of immune complexes and potentially increasing susceptibility to autoimmune diseases (like SLE). While it can increase susceptibility to certain infections, it doesn't specifically explain the susceptibility to *Neisseria* species and the normal C3 levels. C1 deficiency would lead to decreased C4 and C3 consumption, but the vignette explicitly states normal C3 and implies normal C4 (as early components are normal).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | C5 Convertase Deficiency (Correct) | C3 Convertase Deficiency (A) | MBL Deficiency (C) | Properdin Deficiency (D) | C1 Deficiency (E) |
|-------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------|---------------------------|-------------------|
| **Affected Pathway**     | Terminal                          | Early (Classical/Alternative)   | Early (Lectin)     | Early (Alternative)       | Early (Classical)  |
| **Key Defect**          | C5b-9 formation                   | C3b formation                  | Lectin activation  | Alternative activation    | Classical activation |
| **C3 Levels**           | Normal                            | Normal/Low                      | Normal/Low         | Normal/Low                | Normal/Low        |
| **C4 Levels**           | Normal                            | Low (Classical/Lectin)         | Low (Lectin)      | Normal                    | Low               |
| **Primary Susceptibility** | *Neisseria* spp.                  | Encapsulated bacteria          | Encapsulated bacteria | Encapsulated bacteria     | Immune complexes, SLE |
| **Mechanism**           | Impaired MAC formation             | Impaired opsonization           | Impaired activation | Impaired activation        | Impaired activation |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Deficiencies in the terminal complement components (C5-C9) specifically impair the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC), leading to increased susceptibility to infections caused by *Neisseria* species, particularly *N. meningitidis*. Normal levels of early complement components (C3, C4) are characteristic.
- **Differentials:** Consider other causes of recurrent infections (e.g., antibody deficiencies like XLA, phagocytic defects like CGD), but the specific pathogen profile (*Neisseria*) and the isolated terminal complement deficiency strongly point to this diagnosis. Remember that deficiencies in early components (C1, C2, C3, C4, Properdin, MBL) affect multiple pathways and typically present with broader susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, but not the specific *Neisseria* vulnerability seen here.

<a id='question-133'></a>

### Question 133: Graft Rejection & Adaptive Immunity
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cellular and molecular mechanisms underlying acute cellular graft rejection, specifically the role of T lymphocytes (CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and CD4+ helper T cells) in recognizing foreign MHC molecules and mediating tissue damage. (理解移植物急性细胞排斥的细胞和分子机制，特别是T淋巴细胞（CD8+细胞毒性T细胞和CD4+辅助T细胞）在识别外源MHC分子和介导组织损伤中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals diffuse interstitial edema, inflammatory cell infiltrates primarily composed of lymphocytes, and evidence of tubular epithelial cell necrosis. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD3+ T cells, with a predominance of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B. His serum creatinine continues to rise despite dose adjustments of his immunosuppressants.

Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial damage.
(B) CD4+ T helper cells secreting IL-2, which activates B cells to produce anti-donor antibodies.
(C) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes directly killing graft parenchymal cells expressing foreign MHC class I molecules.
(D) Neutrophils recruited by chemokines released from activated macrophages, causing tissue necrosis.
(E) Natural killer (NK) cells recognizing graft cells lacking self-MHC class I molecules.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for graft rejection.
*   **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection.
*   **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction.
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Diffuse interstitial edema, lymphocyte infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell necrosis. This points towards cellular infiltration and direct cell damage within the graft parenchyma.
*   **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. While C4d deposition is often associated with antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), it can also be seen in acute cellular rejection (ACR), particularly in cases with mixed rejection or as a marker of subclinical AMR. However, the predominant histological findings (lymphocyte infiltrates, tubular necrosis) strongly favor ACR.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD3+ T cells, predominance of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B. This is a key finding. CD8+ T cells are cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs), and granzyme B is a key effector molecule used by CTLs to induce apoptosis in target cells.
*   **Immunosuppression:** Standard therapy (cyclosporine, mycophenolate, prednisone) suggests the rejection is occurring despite treatment, indicating a potent immune response.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct cellular mechanism responsible for graft injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** This is a T cell-mediated immune response against the transplanted organ. It typically occurs within days to months post-transplant.
*   **Mechanism of ACR:**
    *   **Sensitization:** Recipient T cells (both CD4+ and CD8+) recognize foreign MHC molecules (alloantigens) on donor antigen-presenting cells (APCs) or graft cells. Donor MHC class I molecules are recognized by recipient CD8+ T cells, and donor MHC class II molecules are recognized by recipient CD4+ T cells.
    *   **Activation:** Antigen recognition leads to T cell activation, proliferation, and differentiation. CD4+ T cells differentiate into helper T cells (Th1, Th2, Th17, etc.), which secrete cytokines (e.g., IL-2, IFN-γ, TNF-α) that amplify the immune response and activate other immune cells (macrophages, B cells, CTLs). CD8+ T cells differentiate into cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
    *   **Effector Phase:**
        *   **CD8+ CTLs:** Directly recognize and kill graft cells (e.g., tubular epithelial cells) presenting foreign MHC class I molecules. They release cytotoxic granules containing perforin (forms pores in target cell membrane) and granzymes (enter target cell via pores and activate caspases, leading to apoptosis). Granzyme B is a serine protease crucial for this process.
        *   **CD4+ T cells:** Can directly participate in graft injury by secreting cytokines that activate macrophages and other inflammatory cells, or by providing help to B cells (though B cells are less central in pure ACR).
        *   **Macrophages:** Activated by cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ from Th1 cells), they contribute to inflammation and tissue damage.
*   **Flow Cytometry Interpretation:** The finding of increased CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B strongly implicates CTL-mediated cytotoxicity as the primary mechanism of graft injury.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial damage. This describes Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR). While C4d deposition can be seen, the predominant histological findings (lymphocyte infiltrates causing tubular necrosis) and the flow cytometry data (high CD8+ T cells with granzyme B) are more characteristic of ACR. AMR typically involves antibodies against donor HLA or non-HLA antigens, leading to complement activation, endothelial injury (endotheliitis), and microvascular inflammation.
- (B) CD4+ T helper cells secreting IL-2, which activates B cells to produce anti-donor antibodies. This describes a component of humoral immunity and potentially AMR. While CD4+ T cells are involved in ACR (secreting cytokines like IL-2 and IFN-γ), their primary role in ACR is often activating CD8+ T cells and macrophages, rather than directly driving B cell antibody production against the graft in this acute setting. The key effector cells causing direct parenchymal damage in this scenario are CD8+ CTLs.
- (D) Neutrophils recruited by chemokines released from activated macrophages, causing tissue necrosis. This describes a mechanism more typical of acute inflammation or certain types of graft injury, but not the primary mechanism of classic ACR. While macrophages and neutrophils can be present in ACR, the dominant effector cells causing direct cell killing are T lymphocytes, specifically CD8+ CTLs.
- (E) Natural killer (NK) cells recognizing graft cells lacking self-MHC class I molecules. NK cells can play a role in graft rejection, particularly in cases where graft cells downregulate MHC class I expression to evade CTLs. However, NK cell activity is typically inhibited by the presence of self-MHC class I molecules. In the context of a standard kidney transplant rejection with high CD8+ T cell activity and granzyme B expression, CTL-mediated cytotoxicity is the more direct and prominent mechanism.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AMR)                                  |
| :------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Mediators** | CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs), CD4+ Helper T cells      | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSA), Complement, NK cells           |
| **Target**          | Graft Parenchymal Cells (e.g., tubules), Endothelium           | Endothelium (microvasculature)                                  |
| **Histology**       | Lymphocyte infiltrates (interstitial, tubules), cell necrosis | Endotheliitis, Microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition      |
| **Key Cells**       | CD8+ T cells (granzyme B+, perforin+), CD4+ T cells (IFN-γ+) | B cells, Plasma cells, Macrophages, NK cells                   |
| **Timeline**        | Days to months                                                 | Days to months                                                 |
| **Flow Cytometry**  | Increased CD8+ T cells, Granzyme B expression                 | Increased B cells, Plasma cells (less common in acute phase) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by T lymphocytes, particularly CD8+ cytotoxic T cells recognizing foreign MHC class I on graft cells and inducing apoptosis via granzyme B and perforin. Differentiating ACR from AMR is crucial, as treatment strategies differ. Key features favoring ACR include prominent T cell infiltrates, parenchymal cell necrosis, and high CD8+ T cell activity with granzyme B expression. AMR is characterized by endothelial injury, microvascular inflammation, C4d deposition, and the presence of DSAs.

<a id='question-134'></a>

### Question 134: Graft Rejection & Immune Cell Phenotyping
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating types of graft rejection (hyperacute, acute, chronic) based on timing, mechanism (antibody-mediated vs. T-cell mediated), and histological/immunological findings, specifically identifying the key immune cell type involved in acute cellular rejection. (区分移植物排斥类型（超急性、急性、慢性）的时程、机制（抗体介导 vs T细胞介导）和组织学/免疫学发现，特别是识别参与急性细胞性排斥的关键免疫细胞类型。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltrates, and tubular epithelial cell damage. Immunofluorescence staining shows C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing HLA-DR and CD69. Which of the following immune cell types is primarily responsible for the observed graft injury in this patient?

(A) CD4+ T helper cells
(B) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes
(C) B lymphocytes producing anti-HLA antibodies
(D) Natural killer (NK) cells
(E) Macrophages

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for graft rejection.
- **Timing:** Three weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is characteristic of acute rejection, specifically acute cellular rejection (ACR) or acute antibody-mediated rejection (AAMR). Hyperacute rejection occurs within minutes to hours, and chronic rejection develops over months to years.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine. These are classic signs of acute graft dysfunction.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, lymphocytic infiltrates, tubular epithelial cell damage. This indicates inflammation and direct injury to the graft parenchyma.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. C4d is a stable breakdown product of the classical complement pathway activation, strongly suggesting antibody-mediated injury (AAMR). However, the question asks about the *primary* cell type responsible for the *observed graft injury*, and the flow cytometry data points strongly towards a cellular component. C4d deposition can sometimes be seen in ACR as well, or represent a mixed rejection picture.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing HLA-DR and CD69. CD8+ T cells are cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). HLA-DR expression indicates activation, and CD69 is an early activation marker. This strongly implicates activated CD8+ T cells in the rejection process.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *primary* immune cell type responsible for the graft injury. Given the timing (acute), biopsy findings (lymphocytic infiltrate, tubular damage), and especially the flow cytometry data (activated CD8+ T cells), acute cellular rejection mediated by CD8+ CTLs is the most likely mechanism. While C4d suggests AAMR, the prominent CD8+ T cell activation points towards a significant cellular component, often the dominant feature in ACR.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Graft Rejection Types:**
    - **Hyperacute:** Occurs minutes to hours post-transplant due to pre-formed anti-HLA antibodies (e.g., ABO incompatibility, prior sensitization). Mediated by complement activation and NK cells. Characterized by thrombosis and necrosis.
    - **Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR):** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes) recognizing donor HLA antigens. CD8+ CTLs directly kill graft cells expressing donor MHC class I. CD4+ T cells help activate CTLs and macrophages. Histology shows lymphocytic infiltrates, interstitial edema, and tubular damage.
    - **Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AAMR):** Occurs days to weeks post-transplant. Mediated by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens on endothelial cells. Activates complement (leading to C4d deposition) and recruits inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages). Histology shows microvascular inflammation (glomerulitis, peritubular capillaritis), thrombosis, and endothelial injury.
    - **Chronic Rejection:** Occurs months to years post-transplant. Characterized by fibrosis, vascular changes (graft arteriosclerosis), and progressive loss of graft function. Involves both cellular and humoral immunity.
- **CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs):** Recognize peptide antigens presented by MHC class I molecules on target cells. Upon activation (e.g., by encountering donor HLA on graft cells), they release cytotoxic granules (perforin, granzymes) and express Fas ligand (FasL), inducing apoptosis in target cells. They are key mediators of acute cellular rejection.
- **CD4+ T Helper Cells:** Recognize peptide antigens presented by MHC class II molecules. They help activate CTLs (by providing co-stimulation and cytokines like IL-2) and B cells. They also contribute to inflammation by releasing cytokines.
- **B Lymphocytes:** Produce antibodies, including DSAs that mediate AAMR.
- **NK Cells:** Involved in hyperacute rejection and can contribute to AAMR (antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity, ADCC).
- **Macrophages:** Phagocytic cells involved in inflammation and tissue damage in various types of rejection.

##### 3. Flow Cytometry Interpretation:**
- **CD8+ T cells:** Cytotoxic T lymphocytes.
- **HLA-DR:** MHC class II molecule expressed on activated antigen-presenting cells (APCs) and activated T cells. Its expression on CD8+ T cells indicates activation.
- **CD69:** An early activation marker expressed on lymphocytes upon stimulation.
- The finding of increased activated CD8+ T cells strongly supports the role of cellular immunity, specifically CTLs, in the graft injury.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **CD4+ T helper cells:** While CD4+ T cells play a role in activating CTLs and contributing to inflammation in ACR, they are not the primary effector cells directly killing graft cells. The flow cytometry specifically highlights CD8+ T cell activation.
- (C) **B lymphocytes producing anti-HLA antibodies:** This describes the mechanism of AAMR. While C4d deposition suggests an antibody-mediated component, the prominent CD8+ T cell activation identified by flow cytometry points towards ACR being a major, if not primary, contributor to the injury in this specific case. The question asks for the *primary* cell type responsible for the *observed* injury, and the flow data strongly implicates CD8+ T cells.
- (D) **Natural killer (NK) cells:** NK cells are primarily involved in hyperacute rejection and ADCC in AAMR. They are not the main mediators of acute cellular rejection.
- (E) **Macrophages:** Macrophages contribute to inflammation and tissue damage in various types of rejection but are not the primary specific effector cells targeting graft cells in ACR.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute Cellular Rejection (ACR) | Acute Antibody-Mediated Rejection (AAMR) | Hyperacute Rejection | Chronic Rejection |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------------------------|----------------------|-------------------|
| **Timing**           | Days to weeks                   | Days to weeks                            | Minutes to hours     | Months to years   |
| **Primary Mediator** | T lymphocytes (CD8+ CTLs)        | Donor-Specific Antibodies (DSAs)         | Pre-formed Abs       | T & B cells, Fibrosis |
| **Key Cells**        | CD8+ T cells, CD4+ T cells       | B cells, Endothelial cells, Neutrophils | NK cells, Neutrophils | Macrophages, Fibroblasts |
| **Mechanism**        | Direct cell killing, Inflammation | Complement activation, ADCC, Inflammation | Complement, ADCC    | Fibrosis, Vascular changes |
| **Histology**        | Lymphocytic infiltrates, Tubule damage | Microvascular inflammation, Thrombosis | Thrombosis, Necrosis | Fibrosis, Arteriosclerosis |
| **Immuno. Findings** | Activated CD8+ T cells           | C4d deposition, DSAs                     | Pre-formed Abs       | Variable          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute graft rejection (occurring weeks post-transplant) can be mediated by T cells (ACR) or antibodies (AAMR). ACR is characterized by T cell infiltrates and direct cell killing, often dominated by activated CD8+ CTLs. AAMR is characterized by antibody deposition (C4d), microvascular inflammation, and endothelial injury. Flow cytometry can help differentiate by identifying activated T cell populations (ACR) or confirming the presence of DSAs (AAMR). Key differentials include hyperacute rejection (immediate, pre-formed antibodies) and chronic rejection (late onset, fibrosis).

<a id='question-135'></a>

### Question 135: Immunodeficiency & Opportunistic Infection
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of specific immunodeficiencies, particularly defects in T-cell function (specifically CD4+ T cells and their role in cell-mediated immunity), and the resulting susceptibility to specific opportunistic infections. It also assesses the ability to interpret clinical presentation and laboratory findings to identify the underlying immune defect and causative pathogen. (考察特定免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞功能缺陷（特别是CD4+ T细胞及其在细胞介导免疫中的作用），以及由此产生的对特定机会性感染的易感性。同时考察根据临床表现和实验室结果解释潜在免疫缺陷和致病原的能力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of progressively worsening fever, non-productive cough, and exertional dyspnea. He reports recent weight loss and night sweats. His medical history is significant for recurrent sinopulmonary infections since childhood, including multiple episodes of pneumonia requiring hospitalization. He denies intravenous drug use, recent travel, or contact with known tuberculosis cases. Physical examination reveals a cachectic male with a temperature of 38.5°C, respiratory rate of 24 breaths/min, and bibasilar crackles on auscultation. Chest X-ray shows diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. Sputum analysis reveals numerous acid-fast bacilli (AFB) on Ziehl-Neelsen staining. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid culture grows *Mycobacterium avium* complex (MAC). Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a markedly reduced number of CD4+ T cells (absolute count 150 cells/µL) and normal numbers of CD8+ T cells, B cells, and NK cells. Serum immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) are within normal limits.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Defect in complement component C3
(B) Defect in B-cell maturation leading to agammaglobulinemia
(C) Defect in T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling
(D) Defect in phagocyte chemotaxis
(E) Defect in CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male with recurrent sinopulmonary infections since childhood. This suggests a chronic, congenital immunodeficiency.
*   **Current Presentation:** Fever, cough, dyspnea, weight loss, night sweats, bibasilar crackles, diffuse interstitial infiltrates on CXR. This points towards a severe, systemic infection, likely opportunistic.
*   **Microbiological Findings:** Sputum AFB positive, BAL culture positive for *Mycobacterium avium* complex (MAC). MAC is a common opportunistic pathogen, particularly in patients with impaired cell-mediated immunity.
*   **Immunological Findings:** Markedly reduced CD4+ T cells (absolute count 150 cells/µL), normal CD8+, B cells, NK cells, and normal immunoglobulin levels. This specific pattern strongly suggests a defect primarily affecting CD4+ T-cell function or number.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely underlying immunological defect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **CD4+ T cells:** These are crucial for cell-mediated immunity, particularly in activating macrophages to kill intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium*. They recognize antigens presented on MHC class II molecules.
*   **MAC Infection:** *Mycobacterium avium* complex is an intracellular bacterium. Effective clearance requires activated macrophages, which are primarily activated by CD4+ T cells via cytokines like IFN-γ. Patients with low CD4+ T cell counts (e.g., advanced HIV infection, certain primary immunodeficiencies) are highly susceptible to disseminated MAC infection.
*   **Differential Diagnosis of Low CD4+ T cells:**
    *   **HIV infection:** The most common cause of severe CD4+ T-cell deficiency in adults. However, the patient denies risk factors (IV drug use, travel, TB contact) and has had symptoms since childhood.
    *   **Primary Immunodeficiencies:** Several genetic defects can lead to low CD4+ T cells.
        *   **Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID):** Usually presents earlier in infancy with severe infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, opportunistic). Often involves defects in both T and B cells. This patient has normal B cells and Ig levels.
        *   **DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion):** Characterized by thymic hypoplasia/aplasia leading to T-cell deficiency (often both CD4+ and CD8+), hypoparathyroidism, and congenital heart defects. While T-cell deficiency is present, the specific pattern (isolated low CD4+) is less typical.
        *   **Hyper-IgE Syndrome (Job's Syndrome):** Primarily affects T-cell function (Th17 pathway), leading to recurrent staphylococcal infections, eczema, and high IgE. CD4+ counts are usually normal or slightly reduced.
        *   **CD40 Ligand Deficiency (X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome):** CD40L is expressed on activated T cells and is required for T-cell help to B cells (for class switching and affinity maturation) and for macrophage activation. Deficiency leads to normal or elevated IgM, low IgG/IgA/IgE, and susceptibility to opportunistic infections (like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*, CMV, MAC) due to impaired macrophage activation by CD4+ T cells. The patient has normal Ig levels, which makes this less likely *unless* the defect is primarily in macrophage activation rather than B-cell help. However, the profound CD4+ lymphopenia is the most striking feature.
        *   **Defects in TCR signaling:** Can lead to impaired T-cell development or function, potentially causing low CD4+ counts.
        *   **Defects in MHC Class II expression/antigen presentation:** Can impair CD4+ T-cell activation.
*   **Evaluating the Options:**
    *   (A) Complement defect (C3): Leads to susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Neisseria*, *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*). Does not typically cause low CD4+ counts or MAC infection.
    *   (B) B-cell maturation defect (agammaglobulinemia): Leads to low levels of all immunoglobulins and susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria. T-cell numbers and function are usually normal. This patient has normal Ig levels.
    *   (C) Defect in TCR signaling: Could potentially lead to low CD4+ counts and impaired T-cell function, increasing susceptibility to intracellular pathogens. This is a possible cause but less specific than CD40L deficiency given the clinical picture.
    *   (D) Defect in phagocyte chemotaxis (e.g., Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency): Leads to recurrent bacterial infections, impaired wound healing, and delayed umbilical cord separation. Does not typically cause low CD4+ counts or MAC infection.
    *   (E) Defect in CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression: CD40L on activated CD4+ T cells binds to CD40 on macrophages, B cells, and dendritic cells. This interaction is crucial for:
        *   **Macrophage activation:** Essential for killing intracellular pathogens like MAC.
        *   **T-cell dependent B-cell activation:** Required for class switching (IgM to IgG, IgA, IgE) and affinity maturation.
        *   **Dendritic cell maturation:** Important for initiating T-cell responses.
        Deficiency leads to impaired macrophage activation (causing susceptibility to MAC, *Pneumocystis*) and impaired B-cell class switching (leading to hyper-IgM syndrome - high IgM, low IgG/IgA/IgE). While the patient has normal Ig levels, the profound susceptibility to MAC due to impaired macrophage activation mediated by CD4+ T cells (which require CD40L for this function) fits well, especially if the B-cell phenotype is less severe or atypical. The primary defect is often considered a T-cell defect affecting macrophage activation. The low CD4+ count itself might be secondary to the inability of T cells to receive proper signals or function effectively due to the CD40L defect, or it could be a primary T-cell developmental issue linked to the same genetic defect. Given the options, CD40L deficiency is the best fit for severe opportunistic infection (MAC) linked to impaired T-cell function affecting macrophage activation, even with normal Ig levels.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defect in complement component C3:** C3 deficiency impairs opsonization and lysis, leading to recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Neisseria meningitidis*, *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*). It does not directly cause T-cell lymphopenia or susceptibility to intracellular pathogens like MAC. The patient's presentation (MAC infection, low CD4+ count) is inconsistent with a primary C3 defect.
- (B) **Defect in B-cell maturation leading to agammaglobulinemia:** This results in a lack of mature B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia (low IgG, IgA, IgM). Patients are susceptible to extracellular bacterial infections, especially encapsulated organisms. T-cell function is typically normal. The patient has normal immunoglobulin levels and a primary T-cell defect (low CD4+ count), making this incorrect.
- (C) **Defect in T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling:** Defects in TCR signaling pathways (e.g., ZAP-70 deficiency) can impair T-cell development and function, leading to T-cell lymphopenia and susceptibility to infections. While this could cause low CD4+ counts and opportunistic infections, CD40L deficiency provides a more specific explanation for the combination of severe opportunistic infection (MAC) and impaired T-cell-mediated macrophage activation.
- (D) **Defect in phagocyte chemotaxis:** Conditions like Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (LAD) impair the ability of phagocytes (neutrophils) to migrate to sites of infection. This leads to recurrent bacterial infections, often without pus formation, impaired wound healing, and delayed umbilical cord separation. It does not cause T-cell lymphopenia or susceptibility to MAC.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CD40L Deficiency (X-linked Hyper-IgM) | C3 Deficiency | Agammaglobulinemia (B-cell defect) | Phagocyte Chemotaxis Defect (e.g., LAD) | TCR Signaling Defect |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :----------- | :-------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | CD40L on T cells                     | C3 protein   | B-cell maturation                | Phagocyte adhesion/chemotaxis           | TCR signaling       |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | T cells (function), B cells (help), Macrophages | N/A          | B cells                          | Phagocytes (neutrophils)               | T cells              |
| **Ig Levels**           | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE (typical) or Normal (atypical) | Normal       | Low IgG, IgA, IgM                 | Normal                                  | Normal               |
| **CD4+ T Cell Count**   | Often Low                            | Normal       | Normal                            | Normal                                  | Often Low            |
| **Key Infections**      | MAC, *Pneumocystis*, CMV, Cryptosporidium | Encapsulated bacteria | Encapsulated bacteria             | Bacterial (no pus), fungal             | Opportunistic        |
| **Mechanism of Susceptibility** | Impaired macrophage activation, impaired B-cell class switch | Impaired opsonization/lysis       | Lack of antibodies                     | Impaired bacterial clearance           | Impaired T-cell function |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The combination of recurrent opportunistic infections (especially MAC), markedly reduced CD4+ T cells, and normal immunoglobulin levels strongly points towards a defect in T-cell function, specifically the ability of CD4+ T cells to activate macrophages. CD40L deficiency is a classic example of such a defect, leading to impaired macrophage activation and often associated with low CD4+ counts, despite typically causing hyper-IgM syndrome. Key differentials include HIV (less likely given history), other primary T-cell defects, and severe B-cell defects (ruled out by normal Igs). Remember that CD4+ T cells are critical for orchestrating cell-mediated immunity against intracellular pathogens.

<a id='question-136'></a>

### Question 136: Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of acute Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD), specifically the roles of donor T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) in recognizing host antigens as foreign and mediating tissue damage, particularly in the skin, liver, and gastrointestinal tract. (理解移植物抗宿主病 (GVHD) 的发病生理机制，特别是供者 T 细胞 (CD8+ 细胞毒性 T 淋巴细胞和 CD4+ 辅助 T 细胞) 在识别宿主抗原为外来物并介导组织损伤中的作用，尤其是在皮肤、肝脏和胃肠道。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) undergoes allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) from a matched unrelated donor. His conditioning regimen includes fludarabine and total body irradiation. Post-transplant, he develops a maculopapular rash starting on his trunk and spreading centrifugally, accompanied by watery diarrhea, and mild jaundice. Liver function tests show elevated bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase. A skin biopsy reveals apoptosis of keratinocytes in the basal layer and lymphocytic infiltrate. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD3+ cells, with a predominance of CD8+ T cells. The patient is diagnosed with acute GVHD. Which of the following cellular mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the observed skin rash in this patient?

(A) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) mediated by donor NK cells recognizing host MHC class I molecules.
(B) Direct cytotoxicity mediated by donor CD8+ T cells recognizing host MHC class I molecules presenting self-antigens.
(C) Indirect cytotoxicity mediated by donor CD4+ T cells recognizing host antigen-presenting cells (APCs) presenting host antigens via host MHC class II molecules.
(D) Complement-dependent cytotoxicity (CDC) mediated by donor antibodies against host endothelial cells.
(E) Type III hypersensitivity reaction involving deposition of immune complexes in dermal blood vessels.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male with AML undergoing allogeneic HSCT. This sets the stage for potential GVHD.
- **Conditioning Regimen:** Fludarabine and total body irradiation. This regimen is immunosuppressive but doesn't eliminate all host immune cells or prevent donor T cell activation.
- **Symptoms:** Maculopapular rash (trunk to extremities), watery diarrhea, mild jaundice. These are classic signs of acute GVHD affecting the skin, GI tract, and liver, respectively.
- **Skin Biopsy:** Apoptosis of keratinocytes in the basal layer and lymphocytic infiltrate. This histological finding is characteristic of GVHD-induced skin damage.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD3+ cells (T cells) with CD8+ predominance. This indicates the presence and activation of donor T cells, specifically cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
- **Diagnosis:** Acute GVHD. The question asks for the mechanism causing the skin rash.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **GVHD Pathophysiology:** Acute GVHD is primarily mediated by donor T cells recognizing host tissues as foreign. This recognition can occur via two main pathways:
    - **Direct Pathway:** Donor T cells (both CD8+ CTLs and CD4+ helper T cells) directly recognize intact host MHC molecules (MHC class I and MHC class II) on host APCs and other tissue cells as foreign antigens. This is the predominant mechanism for acute GVHD. CD8+ T cells recognize host MHC class I, leading to direct killing of target cells (like keratinocytes in the skin). CD4+ T cells recognize host MHC class II, leading to helper functions that amplify the immune response.
    - **Indirect Pathway:** Donor APCs process host antigens and present them via host MHC molecules (MHC class II) to donor CD4+ T cells. This pathway is thought to be more important in chronic GVHD.
- **Skin Rash Mechanism:** The maculopapular rash in acute GVHD is caused by the cytotoxic effects of donor T cells on host keratinocytes and endothelial cells in the skin. CD8+ T cells are the primary mediators of this direct cytotoxicity against keratinocytes expressing host MHC class I.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) ADCC: NK cells are involved in GVHD, but ADCC is not the primary mechanism for the skin rash. NK cells typically recognize cells lacking MHC class I or expressing stress ligands, not intact host MHC class I.
    - (B) Direct cytotoxicity by CD8+ T cells: This aligns perfectly with the pathophysiology of acute GVHD, where donor CD8+ T cells recognize host MHC class I on keratinocytes and directly kill them, causing the characteristic rash. The flow cytometry showing CD8+ predominance supports this.
    - (C) Indirect cytotoxicity by CD4+ T cells: While CD4+ T cells play a role in GVHD (especially in amplifying the response and contributing to chronic GVHD), the direct killing of keratinocytes causing the acute skin rash is primarily mediated by CD8+ T cells via the direct pathway.
    - (D) CDC: Antibody-mediated complement activation is not the primary mechanism of acute GVHD skin damage.
    - (E) Type III hypersensitivity: Immune complex deposition is not characteristic of GVHD pathogenesis. GVHD is a cell-mediated immune response.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. While donor NK cells can contribute to GVHD, their primary mechanism is not ADCC against host MHC class I. ADCC involves antibodies coating target cells, which is not the main process here. The direct pathway involving T cells is more central to the acute skin rash.
- (B): Correct. This accurately describes the direct pathway of acute GVHD, where donor CD8+ T cells recognize host MHC class I on target cells like keratinocytes and induce apoptosis, leading to the skin rash. The clinical presentation and flow cytometry findings support this mechanism.
- (C): Incorrect. Indirect presentation by APCs is more relevant to chronic GVHD and involves CD4+ T cells recognizing processed host antigens. While CD4+ T cells are involved in acute GVHD, the direct killing of keratinocytes causing the rash is primarily attributed to CD8+ T cells via the direct pathway.
- (D): Incorrect. Complement-dependent cytotoxicity requires antibodies, which are not the primary mediators of the cellular damage in acute GVHD. GVHD is fundamentally a T cell-mediated process.
- (E): Incorrect. Type III hypersensitivity involves immune complex deposition, which is not the mechanism underlying the T cell-mediated destruction of keratinocytes seen in GVHD.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute GVHD (Skin Rash)                                  | Chronic GVHD (Skin)                                     | Type IV Hypersensitivity (e.g., Contact Dermatitis) | Autoimmune Blistering Disease (e.g., Pemphigus) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediator** | Donor CD8+ T cells (Direct Pathway)                     | Donor T cells (Direct & Indirect Pathways), Fibrosis     | Donor CD4+ T cells (Delayed Type)                  | Autoantibodies against desmosomal proteins        |
| **Target Antigen**   | Host MHC Class I on keratinocytes                       | Host & Donor Antigens (often minor histocompatibility)   | Haptens bound to self-proteins                   | Desmoglein 1/3                                     |
| **Mechanism**        | Direct Cytotoxicity (Granzyme/Perforin)                  | Cytotoxicity, Fibrosis, Autoimmunity                    | Macrophage activation, Cytokine release            | Antibody-mediated disruption of cell adhesion     |
| **Histology**        | Keratinocyte apoptosis, Lymphocytic infiltrate (basal layer) | Lichenoid inflammation, Atrophy, Fibrosis               | Lymphocytic infiltrate, Spongiosis                 | Intraepidermal blisters, Acantholysis             |
| **Timing**           | Days to weeks post-HSCT                                  | Months to years post-HSCT                                 | Days post-exposure                                 | Variable                                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute GVHD is primarily a T cell-mediated process where donor T cells recognize host tissues as foreign. The direct pathway, involving CD8+ T cells recognizing host MHC class I, is the main mechanism for acute skin GVHD. Differentiate GVHD from other causes of post-transplant rash, such as infection or drug reactions, and understand the key immunological pathways involved. Remember that chronic GVHD often involves indirect pathways and fibrosis, while acute GVHD is characterized by direct T cell cytotoxicity.

<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: The Immune System: Part 3 Focus 37 (infection.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis of opportunistic fungal infections, specifically *Aspergillus* species, in immunocompromised patients, particularly those with neutropenia, and recognizing the characteristic histopathological findings. (理解机会性真菌感染，特别是 *曲霉菌* 属，在免疫功能低下患者中的发病机制，特别是中性粒细胞减少症患者，并识别其特征性组织病理学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male patient with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) is undergoing induction chemotherapy. He develops fever, cough, and pleuritic chest pain. His absolute neutrophil count (ANC) is 150 cells/µL (normal >1500 cells/µL). A chest X-ray reveals multiple, poorly defined, nodular opacities bilaterally, some with cavitation. A computed tomography (CT) scan of the chest shows these nodules with a surrounding "halo sign" (ground-glass opacity surrounding the nodule). Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis shows septate hyphae branching at acute angles (45 degrees) on Gomori methenamine silver (GMS) stain. A lung biopsy is performed, revealing invasive fungal hyphae within blood vessel walls, causing transmural necrosis and infarction. Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect predisposing this patient to this specific infection?

(A) Deficiency in complement component C3
(B) Deficiency in CD4+ T cells
(C) Deficiency in phagocytic cell function (neutropenia)
(D) Deficiency in antibody production (agammaglobulinemia)
(E) Deficiency in cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) function

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male with AML undergoing induction chemotherapy. This immediately flags him as severely immunocompromised. Chemotherapy, especially induction therapy for AML, is known to cause profound myelosuppression.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, cough, pleuritic chest pain. These are classic symptoms of pneumonia or lung infection.
- **Laboratory Finding:** ANC of 150 cells/µL. This indicates severe neutropenia (defined as ANC < 500 cells/µL, and severe neutropenia < 100 cells/µL). Neutrophils are crucial for phagocytosis and killing of bacteria and fungi.
- **Radiological Findings:** Chest X-ray shows multiple nodular opacities, some cavitary. CT scan shows nodules with a "halo sign". The halo sign (ground-glass opacity surrounding a nodule) is highly suggestive of invasive pulmonary aspergillosis in neutropenic patients, representing hemorrhage around the infarcted lung tissue.
- **Microbiological Finding:** BAL fluid shows septate hyphae branching at acute angles (45 degrees) on GMS stain. This morphology is characteristic of *Aspergillus* species.
- **Histopathological Finding:** Lung biopsy shows invasive fungal hyphae within blood vessel walls, causing transmural necrosis and infarction. This confirms invasive aspergillosis and demonstrates the angioinvasive nature of the fungus.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely underlying immunological defect predisposing the patient to *this specific infection* (invasive aspergillosis).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Invasive Aspergillosis Pathogenesis:** *Aspergillus* species are ubiquitous molds. In healthy individuals, the immune system effectively controls them. However, in immunocompromised hosts, particularly those with neutropenia, *Aspergillus* can cause invasive disease.
- **Role of Neutrophils:** Neutrophils are the primary host defense against invasive *Aspergillus*. They phagocytose conidia and hyphae and release cytotoxic granules and reactive oxygen species to kill the fungus. Severe neutropenia (<500 cells/µL, especially <100 cells/µL) significantly impairs this defense, allowing *Aspergillus* to invade tissues, particularly blood vessels (angioinvasion), leading to infarction and necrosis.
- **Other Immune Components:** While other immune cells (macrophages, dendritic cells, T cells, B cells) contribute to antifungal immunity, neutrophils are considered the most critical first line of defense against invasive *Aspergillus*.
    - **Macrophages:** Phagocytose fungi, present antigens, and produce cytokines.
    - **Dendritic Cells:** Antigen presentation to T cells.
    - **T cells (CD4+ and CD8+):** Important for cell-mediated immunity and long-term control, but less critical for initial control compared to neutrophils.
    - **B cells/Antibodies:** Play a role, but antibody deficiency is not the primary predisposing factor for invasive aspergillosis.
    - **Complement:** Involved in opsonization and inflammation, but deficiency is not a major risk factor for invasive aspergillosis.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's severe neutropenia (ANC 150 cells/µL) directly correlates with the high risk for invasive aspergillosis, as evidenced by the clinical presentation (fever, cough, chest pain), radiological findings (nodules with halo sign), microbiological findings (septate hyphae at 45 degrees), and histopathology (angioinvasive hyphae).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deficiency in complement component C3: C3 deficiency leads to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Neisseria meningitidis*, *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*) and impaired immune complex clearance. While complement plays a role in antifungal immunity, C3 deficiency is not a primary risk factor for invasive aspergillosis. The clinical picture (neutropenic patient, halo sign, angioinvasive fungus) strongly points towards a neutrophil defect.
- (B) Deficiency in CD4+ T cells: CD4+ T cell deficiency (e.g., HIV/AIDS) primarily increases susceptibility to opportunistic infections like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*, *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*, *Candida* species, and certain viruses (CMV). While T cells are involved in antifungal immunity, severe neutropenia is a much stronger and more specific risk factor for invasive aspergillosis than CD4+ T cell deficiency.
- (D) Deficiency in antibody production (agammaglobulinemia): Agammaglobulinemia (e.g., X-linked agammaglobulinemia) leads to recurrent bacterial infections, particularly with encapsulated bacteria. Antibody responses are less critical for controlling invasive *Aspergillus* compared to phagocytic cells like neutrophils.
- (E) Deficiency in cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) function: CTL deficiency impairs the control of intracellular pathogens (viruses, some intracellular bacteria) and tumor surveillance. It is not a primary risk factor for invasive fungal infections like aspergillosis, which are primarily controlled by phagocytes.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Invasive Aspergillosis (Neutropenia) | Invasive Candidiasis (T-cell/Neutropenia) | Pneumocystis Pneumonia (T-cell) | Bacterial Infections (Agammaglobulinemia) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Risk Factor** | Severe Neutropenia (<100 cells/µL)  | T-cell deficiency, Neutropenia           | CD4+ T-cell deficiency (<200)     | B-cell/Antibody deficiency                 |
| **Typical Patient**     | Chemotherapy, Hematopoietic stem cell transplant | Broad-spectrum antibiotics, TPN, Central lines, Immunosuppression | HIV/AIDS, Immunosuppression              | Genetic defects (e.g., XLA)                |
| **Microorganism**       | *Aspergillus* spp. (mold)             | *Candida* spp. (yeast)                    | *Pneumocystis jirovecii* (fungus) | Encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *S. pneumo*) |
| **Morphology**          | Septate hyphae, 45° branching         | Pseudohyphae, budding yeast              | Cyst forms on GMS stain           | Variable (cocci, rods)                     |
| **Key Pathology**       | Angioinvasion, infarction, necrosis   | Tissue invasion, abscess formation        | Interstitial pneumonitis          | Pyogenic inflammation                      |
| **Radiology**           | Halo sign, cavitation                | Variable                                 | Ground-glass opacities            | Variable                                   |
| **Immune Defect**       | Neutrophil dysfunction/deficiency    | T-cell dysfunction/deficiency            | T-cell dysfunction/deficiency    | B-cell/Antibody deficiency                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Invasive aspergillosis is a classic opportunistic infection strongly associated with severe neutropenia, particularly in patients undergoing chemotherapy or hematopoietic stem cell transplantation. Recognizing the characteristic radiological findings (halo sign) and microbiological features (septate hyphae branching at 45 degrees) is crucial. Differentiating it from other opportunistic infections requires understanding the primary immune defects involved (e.g., T-cell defects for *Pneumocystis*, candidiasis; B-cell defects for encapsulated bacteria).
