<a id='question-138'></a>

### Question 138: The Immune System: Part 3 Focus 38 (pathogen)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating between bacterial pathogens based on Gram stain morphology, culture characteristics, biochemical tests, and clinical presentation, specifically focusing on *Klebsiella pneumoniae* and its association with specific clinical syndromes and virulence factors. (区分细菌病原体，基于革兰氏染色形态、培养特性、生化试验和临床表现，特别是 *Klebsiella pneumoniae* 及其与特定临床综合征和毒力因子的关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) and poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of productive cough, fever, chills, and pleuritic chest pain. He is a former smoker (40 pack-years). On examination, he is febrile (38.5°C), tachycardic (110 bpm), and tachypneic (28 breaths/min). Auscultation reveals crackles and bronchial breath sounds in the right lower lobe. A chest X-ray shows consolidation in the right lower lobe, suggestive of pneumonia. Sputum Gram stain reveals Gram-negative rods, some appearing encapsulated. Sputum culture grows lactose-fermenting colonies that are mucoid in appearance. Biochemical testing confirms the organism is positive for urease, indole, and citrate utilization, but negative for oxidase and catalase. The patient is treated empirically with ceftriaxone, but his condition worsens, and he develops septic shock. Subsequent culture reveals resistance to ceftriaxone. Further testing identifies the presence of the *blaKPC* gene.

Which of the following virulence factors is MOST likely responsible for the severe pneumonia and subsequent septic shock in this patient, particularly in the context of his underlying conditions and the observed antibiotic resistance?

(A) Shiga toxin
(B) Exotoxin A
(C) Capsule
(D) Endotoxin (LPS)
(E) Biofilm formation

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with COPD, poorly controlled diabetes, former smoker. These are risk factors for severe bacterial pneumonia, particularly with Gram-negative organisms.
- **Symptoms:** Productive cough, fever, chills, pleuritic chest pain – classic symptoms of pneumonia.
- **Physical Exam:** Fever, tachycardia, tachypnea, crackles/bronchial breath sounds in RLL – consistent with lobar pneumonia.
- **Chest X-ray:** Consolidation in RLL – confirms pneumonia.
- **Sputum Gram Stain:** Gram-negative rods, some encapsulated. This points towards encapsulated Gram-negative bacteria.
- **Sputum Culture:** Lactose-fermenting, mucoid colonies. This is characteristic of *Klebsiella pneumoniae*. Mucoid appearance is often associated with capsule production.
- **Biochemical Tests:** Positive urease, indole, citrate; negative oxidase, catalase. This profile fits *Klebsiella pneumoniae*. (Note: *Klebsiella* is typically oxidase-negative and catalase-positive, but the question states negative catalase, which is unusual. However, the combination of lactose fermentation, mucoid colonies, urease positivity, and clinical context strongly points to *Klebsiella*).
- **Clinical Course:** Worsening condition despite ceftriaxone, development of septic shock. This suggests a virulent organism or antibiotic resistance.
- **Antibiotic Resistance:** Resistance to ceftriaxone, presence of *blaKPC* gene. *blaKPC* encodes a carbapenemase, conferring resistance to carbapenems and often associated with resistance to other beta-lactams like ceftriaxone. This is a hallmark of carbapenem-resistant *Klebsiella pneumoniae* (CRKP).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the virulence factor MOST likely responsible for severe pneumonia and septic shock in this context.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- ***Klebsiella pneumoniae* Virulence:** *K. pneumoniae* is a Gram-negative, encapsulated, facultative anaerobic bacterium. Its primary virulence factor is its polysaccharide capsule.
    - **Capsule:** The capsule is antiphagocytic, preventing opsonization and phagocytosis by neutrophils and macrophages. This allows the bacteria to evade the host's innate immune defenses, leading to increased bacterial load and dissemination. The capsule is a major determinant of virulence, particularly in causing severe pneumonia, bloodstream infections (sepsis), and meningitis. The mucoid appearance of colonies on culture is due to the abundant capsule production.
    - **Endotoxin (LPS):** Like all Gram-negative bacteria, *K. pneumoniae* possesses lipopolysaccharide (LPS) in its outer membrane, which acts as an endotoxin. LPS triggers a strong inflammatory response via TLR4 activation, leading to cytokine release (TNF-α, IL-1, IL-6), fever, vasodilation, increased vascular permeability, and potentially septic shock. While LPS contributes to the inflammatory response and severity of infection, the capsule is considered the primary virulence factor enabling the bacteria to establish and disseminate.
    - **Other Factors:** *K. pneumoniae* also produces siderophores (aerobactin) for iron acquisition and urease, which may contribute to virulence. Biofilm formation can occur but is less emphasized as a primary virulence factor for acute pneumonia compared to the capsule. Exotoxin A is characteristic of *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*, not *Klebsiella*. Shiga toxin is produced by *Shigella dysenteriae* and enterohemorrhagic *E. coli* (EHEC).
- **Clinical Context:** The patient's COPD and diabetes impair lung defenses and immune function, increasing susceptibility to severe pneumonia. The encapsulated nature of *K. pneumoniae* allows it to evade phagocytosis in these compromised hosts. The *blaKPC*-mediated resistance explains the treatment failure with ceftriaxone and the progression to septic shock. The capsule's role in preventing phagocytosis is paramount in establishing the infection and overwhelming the host's defenses, especially in immunocompromised individuals.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Shiga toxin: Produced by *Shigella* and EHEC. Causes dysentery and hemolytic uremic syndrome. Not produced by *Klebsiella pneumoniae* and not relevant to pneumonia/sepsis.
- (B) Exotoxin A: Produced by *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*. Inhibits protein synthesis via ADP-ribosylation of elongation factor 2. Causes various infections, including pneumonia, but is not a virulence factor of *Klebsiella pneumoniae*.
- (D) Endotoxin (LPS): Present in all Gram-negative bacteria, including *Klebsiella*. Contributes significantly to the inflammatory response and septic shock. However, the capsule is considered the *primary* virulence factor that allows *Klebsiella* to establish infection and evade phagocytosis, leading to the high bacterial load seen in severe cases. While LPS is crucial for the systemic inflammatory response, the capsule is key for initial bacterial survival and proliferation within the host. The question asks for the factor *most likely responsible* for the *severe pneumonia and septic shock*, and the capsule's antiphagocytic property is central to *Klebsiella*'s ability to cause these severe outcomes.
- (E) Biofilm formation: While *Klebsiella* can form biofilms, especially on medical devices or in chronic infections, it is not considered the primary virulence factor responsible for acute, severe community-acquired pneumonia leading to septic shock in this patient. The capsule's role in evading phagocytosis is more directly linked to the pathogenesis described.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | *Klebsiella pneumoniae* | *Pseudomonas aeruginosa* | *Shigella dysenteriae* | *E. coli* (EHEC) |
|----------------------|--------------------------|---------------------------|------------------------|-------------------|
| Gram Stain           | Gram-negative rod        | Gram-negative rod         | Gram-negative rod      | Gram-negative rod |
| Morphology           | Encapsulated, mucoid     | Motile, grape-like clusters | Non-motile             | Non-motile        |
| Lactose Fermentation | Positive                 | Negative                  | Negative               | Negative          |
| Urease               | Positive                 | Negative                  | Negative               | Variable          |
| Oxidase              | Negative                 | Positive                  | Negative               | Negative          |
| Key Virulence Factor | Capsule                  | Exotoxin A, LPS, Biofilm  | Shiga toxin            | Shiga toxin       |
| Common Infections    | Pneumonia, UTI, Sepsis   | Pneumonia, UTI, Burn wounds | Dysentery              | Hemorrhagic colitis |
| *blaKPC* Gene        | Yes (CRKP)               | No                        | No                     | No                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The polysaccharide capsule is the primary virulence factor of *Klebsiella pneumoniae*, enabling it to evade phagocytosis and cause severe infections like pneumonia and sepsis, especially in immunocompromised hosts. Differentiate *Klebsiella* from other Gram-negative rods based on its mucoid colonies, lactose fermentation, urease positivity, and capsule production. Remember that while LPS contributes to sepsis, the capsule is key for establishing the infection. Key differentials include *Pseudomonas aeruginosa* (Exotoxin A, oxidase positive) and *Shigella/EHEC* (Shiga toxin).

<a id='question-139'></a>

### Question 139: Immunodeficiency & Opportunistic Infections
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of specific immunodeficiencies, particularly combined immunodeficiencies affecting both T and B cell function, and their association with specific opportunistic infections. It requires differentiating between various primary immunodeficiencies based on clinical presentation and laboratory findings. (本题考察特定免疫缺陷，特别是影响T细胞和B细胞功能的联合免疫缺陷，以及它们与特定机会性感染的相关性。需要根据临床表现和实验室结果区分各种原发性免疫缺陷。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy, born to consanguineous parents, presents with recurrent severe bacterial infections (pneumonia, otitis media, sinusitis) starting at 6 months of age, failure to thrive, and chronic diarrhea. He has never received any vaccinations. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory tests show markedly reduced numbers of peripheral blood lymphocytes (absolute lymphocyte count 500/µL), absent T cell-dependent antibody responses to polysaccharide antigens (e.g., Pneumococcus), and absent delayed-type hypersensitivity (DTH) skin reactions to Candida albicans. Flow cytometry reveals a severe deficiency of both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, but normal numbers of B cells and NK cells. B cells fail to undergo class switching and produce primarily IgM antibodies. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *RAG1* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect?

(A) Defective IL-2 receptor signaling leading to impaired T cell proliferation.
(B) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells, preventing T cell help to B cells.
(C) Defective recombination activating genes (RAGs) leading to impaired V(D)J recombination.
(D) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) leading to impaired B cell development.
(E) Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) leading to accumulation of toxic metabolites in lymphocytes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Age of onset (6 months):** Suggests a primary immunodeficiency, as maternal antibodies wane around this time.
*   **Recurrent severe bacterial infections:** Indicates impaired humoral immunity (antibody production) and/or cellular immunity (T cell function).
*   **Failure to thrive, chronic diarrhea:** Common features of severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID).
*   **Consanguineous parents:** Increases the risk of autosomal recessive genetic disorders, common in SCID.
*   **Lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly:** Can be seen in various immunodeficiencies, including SCID, due to chronic infections or lymphoid hyperplasia.
*   **Markedly reduced lymphocytes:** Points towards a severe deficiency affecting lymphocyte numbers or function.
*   **Absent T cell-dependent antibody responses:** Indicates impaired T cell help for B cell activation and class switching.
*   **Absent DTH:** Indicates impaired T cell function.
*   **Flow cytometry: Severe deficiency of CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, normal B cells and NK cells:** This pattern is characteristic of SCID, specifically affecting T cell development.
*   **B cells fail to undergo class switching (produce IgM):** Suggests lack of T cell help, which is required for immunoglobulin class switching in most antibody responses.
*   ***RAG1* gene mutation:** This is the definitive clue. RAG1 and RAG2 are essential for V(D)J recombination, the process by which T cell receptors (TCRs) and B cell receptors (BCRs) are generated.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **V(D)J Recombination:** This process is crucial for the development of functional T cells and B cells. It occurs in the bone marrow (B cells) and thymus (T cells). RAG1 and RAG2 enzymes initiate the process by creating double-strand breaks in DNA at specific recombination signal sequences (RSSs) flanking the variable (V), diversity (D), and joining (J) gene segments. This allows these segments to be joined together, creating a unique antigen receptor.
*   **SCID due to RAG Deficiency:** Mutations in *RAG1* or *RAG2* genes lead to a severe deficiency of both T cells and B cells (T-B-NK+ SCID). While B cells may be present in normal numbers initially, they lack functional BCRs due to failed V(D)J recombination and are non-functional. T cell development is severely impaired in the thymus due to the inability to generate functional TCRs. This results in profound defects in both cellular and humoral immunity, leading to severe susceptibility to opportunistic infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, protozoal) early in life. The clinical picture described (early onset, severe infections, failure to thrive, low lymphocytes, absent T cells, impaired B cell function) is classic for SCID. The *RAG1* mutation confirms this specific type of SCID.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Defective IL-2 receptor signaling leading to impaired T cell proliferation:** This describes X-linked SCID (X-SCID) due to mutations in the *IL2RG* gene, which encodes the common gamma chain (γc) shared by receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. While X-SCID also presents with severe T cell deficiency, the flow cytometry would show absent or severely reduced T cells, and the genetic defect is different (*IL2RG* vs. *RAG1*). The vignette specifically mentions a *RAG1* mutation.
*   **(B) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells, preventing T cell help to B cells:** This describes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal T cell numbers and function but defective T cell help for B cell class switching and somatic hypermutation. This leads to high levels of IgM and low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. Patients are susceptible to opportunistic infections, particularly *Pneumocystis jirovecii*. Flow cytometry would show normal T cell numbers, unlike the vignette.
*   **(D) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) leading to impaired B cell development:** This describes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients have a profound deficiency of B cells and immunoglobulins (all isotypes) due to a block in B cell development in the bone marrow. T cell numbers and function are normal. Patients are susceptible primarily to encapsulated bacteria. Flow cytometry would show absent B cells (CD19+), which contradicts the vignette's finding of normal B cell numbers.
*   **(E) Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) leading to accumulation of toxic metabolites in lymphocytes:** This describes ADA deficiency, another cause of SCID. ADA is an enzyme involved in purine metabolism. Its deficiency leads to the accumulation of deoxyadenosine and deoxy-ATP, which are toxic to lymphocytes, particularly T cells and B cells. This results in a clinical picture similar to RAG deficiency SCID (T-B-NK+ SCID). However, the vignette specifically identifies a *RAG1* mutation, making ADA deficiency incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | RAG1 Deficiency (SCID) | X-SCID (IL2RG) | Hyper-IgM (CD40L) | XLA (BTK) | ADA Deficiency (SCID) |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------- | :------------- | :--------------- | :-------- | :-------------------- |
| **Genetic Defect**      | *RAG1* (or *RAG2*)     | *IL2RG*        | *CD40LG*         | *BTK*     | *ADA*                 |
| **T Cells**             | Absent/Severely ↓     | Absent/Severely ↓ | Normal           | Normal    | Absent/Severely ↓     |
| **B Cells**             | Absent/Non-functional  | Absent/Non-functional | Normal           | Absent    | Absent/Non-functional  |
| **NK Cells**            | Normal                 | Normal         | Normal           | Normal    | Normal                |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | Very Low (all)         | Very Low (all) | High IgM, Low IgG/A/E | Very Low (all) | Very Low (all)         |
| **T Cell Help**         | Absent                 | Absent         | Defective        | Normal    | Absent                 |
| **V(D)J Recombination** | Defective              | Normal         | Normal           | Normal    | Defective              |
| **Key Clue**            | *RAG1* mutation        | *IL2RG* mutation | High IgM         | Absent B cells | Toxic metabolites     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The vignette describes a classic presentation of Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) characterized by early-onset severe infections, failure to thrive, and profound T cell deficiency with normal B and NK cell numbers. The specific genetic defect identified (*RAG1* mutation) points to a defect in V(D)J recombination, which is essential for the development of both T and B cell antigen receptors. Differentiating SCID subtypes (RAG deficiency, IL2RG deficiency, ADA deficiency, etc.) and other primary immunodeficiencies (XLA, Hyper-IgM) requires careful attention to lymphocyte subset numbers, immunoglobulin levels, and specific genetic defects.

<a id='question-140'></a>

### Question 140: Immunodeficiency & Opportunistic Infection
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of specific immunodeficiencies, particularly those affecting T-cell function and antibody production, and their association with susceptibility to specific opportunistic infections. It also assesses the ability to interpret clinical presentation and laboratory findings to identify the underlying immune defect. (本题考察特定免疫缺陷的理解，特别是影响T细胞功能和抗体产生的缺陷，以及它们与特定机会性感染的易感性相关性。它还评估了根据临床表现和实验室结果来识别潜在免疫缺陷的能力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male with a history of recurrent sinopulmonary infections since childhood presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of fever, non-productive cough, and progressive dyspnea. He reports recent travel to a rural area where he was exposed to bird droppings. His past medical history is significant for chronic diarrhea and failure to thrive during infancy. Physical examination reveals diffuse bilateral crackles on lung auscultation and mild splenomegaly. Laboratory studies show a markedly elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) and a chest X-ray consistent with diffuse interstitial infiltrates. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis reveals numerous spherical yeast cells within macrophages. Serum immunoglobulin levels show significantly reduced IgG, IgA, and IgE, with normal IgM. Flow cytometry of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a severely reduced number of CD4+ T cells and normal numbers of CD8+ T cells and B cells. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *CD40LG* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely causative organism responsible for this patient's current pneumonia?

(A) *Pneumocystis jirovecii*
(B) *Cryptococcus neoformans*
(C) *Histoplasma capsulatum*
(D) *Aspergillus fumigatus*
(E) *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections since childhood:** Suggests a congenital immunodeficiency affecting humoral immunity (antibodies) and/or T-cell mediated immunity.
- **Chronic diarrhea and failure to thrive in infancy:** Common manifestations of severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or other primary immunodeficiencies.
- **Exposure to bird droppings:** A significant risk factor for *Histoplasma capsulatum* infection.
- **Fever, cough, dyspnea, diffuse interstitial infiltrates:** Symptoms and radiographic findings consistent with pneumonia, potentially atypical or opportunistic.
- **Spherical yeast cells within macrophages in BAL:** Morphological description characteristic of *Histoplasma capsulatum* within macrophages.
- **Reduced IgG, IgA, IgE, normal IgM:** This pattern suggests a defect in class switching, often associated with T-cell defects affecting B-cell help.
- **Severely reduced CD4+ T cells, normal CD8+ T cells and B cells:** Points towards a specific T-cell defect, particularly affecting helper T cells.
- **Homozygous mutation in the *CD40LG* gene:** This gene encodes CD40 ligand (CD154), which is expressed on activated T cells and is crucial for T-cell dependent B-cell activation, class switching, and macrophage activation. Mutations lead to X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome (HIGM1).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **CD40-CD40L Interaction:** CD40L (CD154) on activated T cells binds to CD40 on B cells, dendritic cells, and macrophages. This interaction is essential for:
    - **T-cell dependent B-cell activation:** Required for isotype switching (from IgM to IgG, IgA, IgE) and affinity maturation.
    - **Germinal center formation:** Crucial for long-term antibody responses.
    - **Macrophage activation:** Enhances macrophage phagocytosis and killing of intracellular pathogens.
- **X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome (HIGM1):** Caused by mutations in *CD40LG*. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but severely reduced IgG, IgA, and IgE due to defective class switching. They also have impaired cell-mediated immunity (due to defective macrophage activation) and are susceptible to opportunistic infections, particularly those caused by intracellular pathogens like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*, *Cryptosporidium*, *Mycobacterium*, and fungi like *Histoplasma*.
- ***Histoplasma capsulatum*:** A dimorphic fungus found in soil contaminated with bird or bat droppings. Inhalation of spores leads to infection. It is an intracellular pathogen, primarily infecting macrophages. The yeast form is typically found within macrophages in tissue. Patients with impaired cell-mediated immunity (like HIGM1) are highly susceptible to disseminated histoplasmosis.
- ***Pneumocystis jirovecii*:** An opportunistic fungus causing pneumonia primarily in patients with severe T-cell deficiency (e.g., AIDS, SCID). While patients with HIGM1 are susceptible, the specific finding of yeast within macrophages and the exposure history strongly point towards *Histoplasma*.
- ***Cryptococcus neoformans*:** An encapsulated yeast causing meningitis and pneumonia, especially in immunocompromised individuals (e.g., HIV/AIDS, transplant recipients). It is typically extracellular or within macrophages but is not the most characteristic finding given the exposure history and specific immunodeficiency.
- ***Aspergillus fumigatus*:** A mold causing invasive aspergillosis, particularly in neutropenic patients or those with chronic granulomatous disease. It is less commonly associated with HIGM1 compared to intracellular pathogens like *Histoplasma*.
- ***Mycobacterium tuberculosis*:** An intracellular bacterium causing tuberculosis. Patients with T-cell defects are at increased risk, but the BAL finding of yeast within macrophages is not typical for *M. tuberculosis*.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) ***Pneumocystis jirovecii*:** While patients with HIGM1 are susceptible to *P. jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP) due to impaired T-cell function, the BAL finding specifically describes spherical yeast cells *within macrophages*, which is more characteristic of *Histoplasma*. PJP typically shows cysts containing sporozoites in BAL. The exposure history also favors *Histoplasma*.
- (B) ***Cryptococcus neoformans*:** This fungus can cause pneumonia and meningitis in immunocompromised hosts. However, it is typically identified by India ink stain (capsule) or culture, and the morphology described (spherical yeast within macrophages) is less specific for *Cryptococcus* compared to *Histoplasma*. The exposure history is also less relevant for *Cryptococcus*.
- (D) ***Aspergillus fumigatus*:** This mold typically causes invasive disease in severely neutropenic patients or those with defects in phagocyte function (e.g., CGD). While immunocompromised patients are at risk, the specific morphology and the patient's immunodeficiency (T-cell/B-cell help defect) make *Aspergillus* less likely than intracellular pathogens like *Histoplasma*.
- (E) ***Mycobacterium tuberculosis*:** This bacterium causes tuberculosis. Patients with T-cell defects are susceptible. However, *M. tuberculosis* is an acid-fast bacillus, not a yeast, and is typically found intracellularly but not described as spherical yeast within macrophages in BAL. The exposure history is not specific for TB.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | *Histoplasma capsulatum* | *Pneumocystis jirovecii* | *Cryptococcus neoformans* | *Aspergillus fumigatus* | *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* | HIGM1 (CD40LG mutation) |
|----------------------|--------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------|--------------------------|------------------------------|--------------------------|
| **Morphology (BAL)** | Yeast within macrophages | Cysts with sporozoites    | Yeast (often encapsulated) | Hyphae, septate          | Acid-fast bacilli            | Reduced IgG/IgA/IgE, Normal IgM, Low CD4+ T cells |
| **Exposure Risk**    | Bird/bat droppings       | Ubiquitous                | Ubiquitous                | Ubiquitous                | Respiratory droplets         | N/A                      |
| **Immune Defect Risk**| Cell-mediated immunity   | T-cell immunity           | T-cell immunity           | Neutrophil function      | T-cell immunity              | T-cell help for B cells, Macrophage activation |
| **Typical Disease**  | Pneumonia, Disseminated  | Pneumonia                 | Meningitis, Pneumonia     | Invasive Aspergillosis   | Pulmonary/Extrapulmonary TB  | Recurrent infections, Opportunistic infections |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Understanding the specific immune defects associated with different primary immunodeficiencies is crucial. X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome (HIGM1) due to *CD40LG* mutations impairs T-cell dependent B-cell activation (leading to low IgG/IgA/IgE, normal IgM) and macrophage activation (due to lack of CD40L-CD40 interaction), predisposing patients to opportunistic infections, especially by intracellular pathogens. The clinical presentation, exposure history, BAL findings, and specific immunoglobulin/lymphocyte profile strongly point to *Histoplasma capsulatum* as the causative agent in this patient with HIGM1. Key differentials include other opportunistic infections seen in T-cell defects like PJP (*Pneumocystis jirovecii*) and fungal infections like *Cryptococcus* and *Aspergillus*, but the specific morphology and exposure history are most consistent with *Histoplasma*.

<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Immune System: Part 3 Focus 41 (anatomic)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical location and function of different lymphoid tissues, specifically the distinction between T cell zones and B cell zones within secondary lymphoid organs, and the role of follicular dendritic cells (FDCs) in B cell activation. (理解不同淋巴组织的位置和功能，特别是二级淋巴器官中T细胞区和B细胞区的区别，以及滤泡树突状细胞 (FDCs) 在B细胞活化中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic lymphocytic leukemia (CLL) presents with progressive fatigue, night sweats, and unexplained weight loss over the past 6 months. He was previously treated with fludarabine and cyclophosphamide, but relapsed 2 years ago. Physical examination reveals generalized lymphadenopathy, particularly prominent in the cervical and axillary regions. A biopsy of an enlarged cervical lymph node is performed. Histopathological analysis shows diffuse effacement of the normal lymph node architecture by a monotonous population of small, mature-appearing lymphocytes. Within the lymph node, distinct areas are identified. One area contains densely packed lymphocytes with irregular nuclear contours and prominent nucleoli, interspersed with histiocytes and occasional plasma cells. Another area shows lymphoid follicles with prominent germinal centers, containing large, pale-staining cells with vesicular nuclei and prominent nucleoli (centroblasts) and smaller, darker-staining cells (centrocytes). Scattered throughout the follicles are stellate-shaped cells with long cytoplasmic processes forming a network, trapping intact immunoglobulin molecules within their meshwork. Flow cytometry of the biopsy specimen confirms the presence of a clonal B-cell population expressing CD5, CD19, CD20 (dim), and CD23, consistent with CLL. The patient's serum immunoglobulins are markedly reduced (hypogammaglobulinemia). Which of the following cell types is primarily responsible for trapping and presenting intact antigen to B cells within the germinal centers of this lymph node?

(A) Marginal zone macrophages
(B) Follicular dendritic cells (FDCs)
(C) Interdigitating dendritic cells (IDCs)
(D) Langerhans cells
(E) M2 macrophages

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with CLL history, relapse, symptoms (fatigue, night sweats, weight loss), generalized lymphadenopathy. This points towards a lymphoproliferative disorder, specifically CLL relapse, potentially with transformation or secondary complications.
- **Lymph Node Biopsy:** Shows effacement of normal architecture by CLL cells (small, mature lymphocytes with CD5+ phenotype). This confirms the underlying CLL.
- **Distinct Areas:** The description highlights two key areas:
    - Area 1: Densely packed lymphocytes with irregular nuclei/nucleoli, histiocytes, plasma cells. This likely represents the CLL infiltrate itself and possibly reactive changes.
    - Area 2: Lymphoid follicles with germinal centers (centroblasts, centrocytes). This indicates reactive follicular hyperplasia, often seen in CLL patients, possibly due to chronic antigenic stimulation or transformation.
- **Stellate Cells:** "Scattered throughout the follicles are stellate-shaped cells with long cytoplasmic processes forming a network, trapping intact immunoglobulin molecules within their meshwork." This is the crucial description of Follicular Dendritic Cells (FDCs).
- **Immunophenotype:** CLL cells are CD5+, CD19+, CD20(dim), CD23+.
- **Hypogammaglobulinemia:** Common in CLL due to impaired B cell function and antibody production.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which cell type traps and presents *intact* antigen to B cells within germinal centers.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Secondary Lymphoid Organs:** Lymph nodes are secondary lymphoid organs where adaptive immune responses are initiated. They have distinct anatomical zones:
    - **T cell zone (paracortex):** Rich in T cells, dendritic cells (specifically IDCs), and high endothelial venules (HEVs). IDCs capture antigens in peripheral tissues, migrate to the T cell zone, process antigens, and present peptides on MHC molecules to T cells.
    - **B cell zone (follicles):** Contains B cells organized into primary follicles and secondary follicles (with germinal centers upon activation).
- **Germinal Centers:** Sites of intense B cell proliferation, somatic hypermutation, affinity maturation, and class switching, occurring within secondary follicles.
- **Follicular Dendritic Cells (FDCs):** Specialized stromal cells located within the B cell follicles and germinal centers. They are *not* of hematopoietic origin (unlike macrophages and dendritic cells).
    - **Function:** FDCs play a critical role in B cell activation and affinity maturation. They trap *intact* antigens (often in the form of immune complexes) on their surface via Fc receptors and complement receptors (CR1, CR2). They then present these intact antigens to B cells, allowing B cells to sample antigens without internalization and processing. This presentation is crucial for selecting B cells with high-affinity receptors during affinity maturation. FDCs also provide survival signals (e.g., BAFF, APRIL) to germinal center B cells.
- **Other Cell Types:**
    - **Marginal zone macrophages:** Located in the marginal sinus of the spleen, trap blood-borne antigens.
    - **Interdigitating dendritic cells (IDCs):** Located in the T cell zone, process antigens, and present peptides on MHC to T cells.
    - **Langerhans cells:** Dendritic cells found in the epidermis, capture antigens in the skin.
    - **M2 macrophages:** Involved in tissue repair and immune regulation, often found in inflamed tissues.

##### 3. Connecting Clues to Answer:
The vignette describes stellate cells in the B cell follicles/germinal centers trapping intact immunoglobulin molecules. This is the classic description and function of Follicular Dendritic Cells (FDCs). The question specifically asks about trapping and presenting *intact* antigen to B cells in germinal centers, which is the primary role of FDCs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Marginal zone macrophages:** Located primarily in the spleen's marginal sinus, not the lymph node follicles. They trap blood-borne antigens, not follicular antigens, and present processed antigens to T cells, not intact antigens to B cells. Incorrect location and function.
- (C) **Interdigitating dendritic cells (IDCs):** Located in the T cell zone (paracortex), not the B cell follicles. They process antigens and present peptides on MHC molecules to T cells. They do not trap intact antigens or primarily interact with B cells in germinal centers. Incorrect location and function.
- (D) **Langerhans cells:** Found in the epidermis, involved in skin immunity. Not relevant to lymph node structure or B cell activation in germinal centers. Incorrect location and function.
- (E) **M2 macrophages:** Involved in tissue repair and immunosuppression. While macrophages can be found in lymph nodes, M2 macrophages are not the specialized cells responsible for trapping intact antigen on FDC networks within germinal centers. Incorrect function.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Cell Type                     | Location                                  | Primary Function                                                                                                | Antigen Presentation | Key Feature                                                                 |
| :---------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Follicular Dendritic Cells (FDCs)** | B cell follicles, Germinal Centers         | Trap intact antigens (immune complexes), present to B cells, provide survival signals to GC B cells.             | Intact Antigen       | Stellate morphology, dense network, non-hematopoietic, CR1/CR2 expression |
| Interdigitating DCs (IDCs)      | T cell zone (Paracortex)                 | Capture, process, and present peptide antigens on MHC to T cells.                                               | Processed Antigen    | Birbeck granules (sometimes), MHC class II expression                      |
| Marginal Zone Macrophages      | Spleen (Marginal Sinus)                   | Trap blood-borne antigens, present to T cells.                                                                 | Processed Antigen    | Location in marginal sinus                                                  |
| Langerhans Cells              | Epidermis                                 | Antigen capture in skin, migrate to lymph nodes, present to T cells.                                             | Processed Antigen    | Birbeck granules, CD1a expression                                          |
| M2 Macrophages                | Various tissues (inflammation, repair) | Phagocytosis, tissue repair, immunosuppression.                                                                 | Processed Antigen    | Arginase-1 expression, CD163, CD206                                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Follicular dendritic cells (FDCs) are unique stromal cells in B cell follicles that trap intact antigens (often as immune complexes) on their surface and present them to B cells, crucial for germinal center reactions and affinity maturation. Differentiate FDCs from other dendritic cell types (IDCs, Langerhans cells) by their location (B cell zone vs. T cell zone/skin) and function (intact antigen presentation vs. processed antigen presentation to T cells). Remember that FDCs are non-hematopoietic.

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Immunodeficiency & Opportunistic Infection
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of specific immunodeficiencies, particularly combined immunodeficiencies affecting both T and B cell function, and their susceptibility to specific opportunistic infections. It also assesses the ability to interpret flow cytometry data related to lymphocyte subsets. (本题考察特定免疫缺陷的理解，特别是影响T细胞和B细胞功能的联合免疫缺陷，以及它们对特定机会性感染的易感性。同时也考察解读与淋巴细胞亚群相关的流式细胞术数据的能力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent severe bacterial infections (pneumonia, otitis media, sinusitis) starting at 6 months of age, failure to thrive, and chronic diarrhea. He has never received any vaccinations. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows markedly reduced numbers of CD3+ T cells, CD4+ T cells, and CD8+ T cells, along with normal numbers of CD19+ B cells and CD16+/CD56+ NK cells. Immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) are severely decreased. The patient is subsequently diagnosed with Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP).

Which of the following immunological defects is most likely responsible for this patient's presentation and susceptibility to PJP?

(A) X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA)
(B) DiGeorge syndrome
(C) Severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) due to adenosine deaminase (ADA) deficiency
(D) Common variable immunodeficiency (CVID)
(E) Chronic granulomatous disease (CGD)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Age of onset (6 months):** Recurrent severe infections starting after the decline of maternal antibodies (around 6 months) suggest a primary immunodeficiency.
*   **Types of infections:** Severe bacterial infections (pneumonia, otitis media, sinusitis) point towards defects in both humoral (B cell) and cellular (T cell) immunity. Chronic diarrhea can also be seen in severe immunodeficiencies.
*   **Failure to thrive:** Common in severe chronic infections and immunodeficiencies.
*   **Lack of vaccinations:** Indicates a lack of protective immunity, consistent with a severe immunodeficiency.
*   **Physical exam:** Lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly can be seen in various immunodeficiencies, including those with lymphocyte proliferation or infiltration.
*   **Flow cytometry:**
    *   Markedly reduced CD3+, CD4+, and CD8+ T cells: Indicates a severe T cell deficiency.
    *   Normal CD19+ B cells: Suggests B cells are present, but their function might be impaired due to lack of T cell help.
    *   Normal CD16+/CD56+ NK cells: Rules out defects primarily affecting NK cells.
*   **Immunoglobulin levels:** Severely decreased IgG, IgA, IgM: Indicates a severe humoral immunodeficiency, likely secondary to the T cell defect (lack of T cell help for B cell maturation and antibody production).
*   **Opportunistic infection (PJP):** *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia is a classic opportunistic infection seen in severe T cell deficiencies.

These findings collectively point towards a severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID), characterized by defects in both T and B cell function, leading to susceptibility to severe bacterial, viral, fungal, and protozoal infections, including PJP.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID):** A group of genetic disorders characterized by profound defects in both T and B lymphocyte development and function. This leads to extreme susceptibility to infections early in life.
*   **ADA Deficiency:** Adenosine deaminase (ADA) is an enzyme involved in purine metabolism. Deficiency leads to the accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine and deoxy-ATP) primarily in lymphocytes, causing their death and resulting in severe lymphopenia, particularly affecting T cells, B cells, and NK cells (though NK cells are often relatively spared compared to T and B cells, as seen in the vignette). This is a common cause of autosomal recessive SCID.
*   **T Cell Function:** T cells are crucial for cell-mediated immunity (killing infected cells, activating macrophages) and for helping B cells produce antibodies. Deficiency leads to susceptibility to intracellular pathogens (viruses, fungi, mycobacteria) and encapsulated bacteria.
*   **B Cell Function:** B cells produce antibodies, essential for humoral immunity against extracellular bacteria and viruses. Deficiency leads to susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.
*   **Pneumocystis jirovecii:** An opportunistic fungus that causes pneumonia primarily in immunocompromised individuals, especially those with T cell defects (like HIV/AIDS or SCID).
*   **Flow Cytometry Interpretation:** CD3 is a pan-T cell marker. CD4 is a helper T cell marker. CD8 is a cytotoxic T cell marker. CD19 is a B cell marker. CD16/CD56 are NK cell markers. The pattern described (low T cells, normal B and NK cells) is characteristic of certain types of SCID, particularly those affecting T cell development or survival.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA):** Caused by a defect in the BTK gene, leading to a block in B cell development. Patients have normal T cell numbers and function but lack B cells and antibodies. They are susceptible to recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated organisms) but typically *not* to opportunistic infections like PJP, which require T cell immunity. Flow cytometry would show absent CD19+ B cells. This doesn't fit the vignette's T cell deficiency and PJP.
*   **(B) DiGeorge syndrome:** Caused by a deletion on chromosome 22q11.2, leading to thymic hypoplasia/aplasia, resulting in T cell deficiency. B cell numbers are usually normal, but antibody production can be impaired due to lack of T cell help. Patients are susceptible to infections, including fungal (Candida) and viral infections. However, the T cell deficiency is variable, and the vignette describes *markedly reduced* T cells across all subsets, which is more typical of SCID than the often milder T cell defect in DiGeorge. Also, DiGeorge syndrome often presents with characteristic facial features, cardiac defects, and hypocalcemia, which are not mentioned here. While PJP can occur, the profound T cell lymphopenia points more strongly to SCID.
*   **(D) Common variable immunodeficiency (CVID):** Characterized by low immunoglobulin levels and impaired antibody responses, leading to recurrent bacterial infections. T cell numbers are usually normal, although T cell function can sometimes be subtly impaired. Onset is typically later than infancy/early childhood. Patients are *not* typically susceptible to PJP unless they develop severe T cell dysfunction later. Flow cytometry would show normal T cell numbers. This doesn't fit the early onset, severe T cell deficiency, and PJP.
*   **(E) Chronic granulomatous disease (CGD):** A defect in NADPH oxidase in phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages), impairing their ability to produce reactive oxygen species to kill certain bacteria and fungi. Patients have recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia cepacia*), often forming granulomas. Lymphocyte numbers and function (T cells, B cells) are typically normal. Flow cytometry would be normal for lymphocytes. This doesn't fit the T cell deficiency, B cell deficiency (low Ig), or PJP.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | SCID (ADA Deficiency) | XLA                 | DiGeorge Syndrome   | CVID                 | CGD                  |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------- | :------------------ | :------------------ | :------------------- | :------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | T & B cell development | B cell development  | T cell development  | B cell function/Ab   | Phagocyte function   |
| **T Cell Count**        | Markedly Reduced      | Normal              | Variable (Reduced)  | Normal               | Normal               |
| **B Cell Count**        | Reduced               | Absent              | Normal              | Normal               | Normal               |
| **Ig Levels**           | Severely Reduced      | Severely Reduced    | Variable (Reduced)  | Severely Reduced    | Normal               |
| **NK Cell Count**       | Variable (Reduced)    | Normal              | Normal              | Normal               | Normal               |
| **Onset**               | Early (infancy)       | Early (infancy)     | Variable (infancy)  | Later (child/adult)  | Early (infancy)      |
| **PJP Susceptibility**  | High                  | Low                 | Moderate            | Low                  | Low                  |
| **Key Infections**      | Broad (bacteria, virus, fungi, protozoa) | Encapsulated bacteria | Fungal, viral        | Encapsulated bacteria | Catalase+ organisms |
| **Flow Cytometry**      | Low T cells           | Absent B cells      | Low T cells         | Normal T/B cells     | Normal lymphocytes   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) presents early in life with failure to thrive and susceptibility to a wide range of infections, including opportunistic ones like PJP, due to profound defects in both T and B cell function. Flow cytometry showing markedly reduced T cells (CD3+, CD4+, CD8+) with normal B and NK cells is highly suggestive of SCID. Differentiating SCID from other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (absent B cells), DiGeorge (variable T cells, often associated features), CVID (later onset, normal T cells), and CGD (phagocyte defect) relies on understanding the specific lymphocyte populations affected and the types of infections encountered.

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Mucosal Immunity & Pathogen Evasion
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of secretory IgA (sIgA) in mucosal immunity and how pathogens can evade this defense mechanism, specifically focusing on enzymatic degradation of antibodies. (理解分泌型IgA (sIgA) 在黏膜免疫中的作用，以及病原体如何通过酶降解抗体来逃避这种防御机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with profuse, watery diarrhea for 3 days following a recent trip to Mexico. He reports consuming undercooked street tacos. He is otherwise healthy with no known immunodeficiencies. Stool cultures reveal a Gram-negative, motile, curved rod bacterium that grows well on selective media containing sodium thiosulfate and ferric citrate. Microscopic examination of the stool shows numerous neutrophils but no red blood cells. The patient's serum IgA levels are normal, but his stool contains significantly lower levels of IgA compared to healthy controls. Further investigation reveals that the isolated bacterium produces an enzyme that specifically cleaves the J chain of IgA molecules.

Which of the following mechanisms best explains the reduced IgA levels in the patient's stool despite normal serum IgA?

(A) Impaired transport of IgA across the intestinal epithelium due to bacterial damage.
(B) Degradation of IgA by bacterial proteases that cleave the Fcα region.
(C) Inhibition of IgA production by plasma cells in the lamina propria.
(D) Prevention of IgA binding to the bacterial surface by the bacterial capsule.
(E) Cleavage of the J chain by bacterial enzymes, preventing proper polymerization and secretion of dimeric IgA.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male, watery diarrhea after travel to Mexico, consumption of undercooked street tacos. This points towards traveler's diarrhea, likely caused by a bacterial pathogen.
*   **Microbiology:** Gram-negative, motile, curved rod, grows on sodium thiosulfate/ferric citrate media. This strongly suggests *Vibrio cholerae* or a related *Vibrio* species (like *Vibrio parahaemolyticus*), common causes of traveler's diarrhea. The presence of neutrophils indicates an inflammatory response, but the absence of RBCs suggests a non-invasive or minimally invasive process.
*   **Immunology:** Normal serum IgA levels but low stool IgA levels. This indicates a problem with IgA function *within the gut lumen*, not systemic IgA production.
*   **Pathogen Mechanism:** The bacterium produces an enzyme that cleaves the J chain of IgA. The J chain is crucial for the polymerization of monomeric IgA into dimeric IgA (secretory IgA or sIgA), which is the form transported across epithelial cells and found in secretions.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Mucosal Immunity:** The gut mucosa is protected by sIgA, which is transported across epithelial cells via the polymeric immunoglobulin receptor (pIgR). Monomeric IgA binds to the pIgR on the basolateral surface of the epithelial cell. The pIgR internalizes the IgA, cleaves off the J chain (using its protease activity), and transports the dimeric IgA (now consisting of two IgA monomers linked by the secretory component, derived from the pIgR) across the cell to the apical surface, where it is released into the lumen.
*   **Role of J Chain:** The J chain is essential for the polymerization of IgA monomers into dimers and trimers. It also facilitates binding to the pIgR.
*   **Bacterial Evasion:** Some bacteria, like certain strains of *Vibrio cholerae* and *Neisseria gonorrhoeae*, produce IgA proteases. These proteases specifically cleave the J chain of IgA. By removing the J chain, the bacteria prevent the formation of dimeric sIgA and its transport across the epithelium, thus evading mucosal immunity. The monomeric IgA remaining in the lumen is less effective at neutralizing pathogens.
*   **Differential Diagnosis:** *Vibrio cholerae* is a classic example of a bacterium producing IgA protease. *Vibrio parahaemolyticus* can also cause traveler's diarrhea and may possess similar virulence factors. Other bacteria like *Haemophilus influenzae*, *Neisseria meningitidis*, and *Neisseria gonorrhoeae* also produce IgA proteases.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired transport of IgA across the intestinal epithelium due to bacterial damage. While some bacteria can damage the epithelium, the primary mechanism described here is enzymatic cleavage of IgA, specifically targeting the J chain, which directly interferes with the *formation* and *secretion* of dimeric sIgA, not just its transport across an intact epithelium. The vignette points to a specific enzymatic activity.
- (B): Degradation of IgA by bacterial proteases that cleave the Fcα region. Cleavage of the Fcα region would degrade the IgA molecule itself, but the vignette specifically states the enzyme cleaves the J chain. IgA proteases typically target the J chain, not the Fc region.
- (C): Inhibition of IgA production by plasma cells in the lamina propria. The patient has normal serum IgA levels, indicating that systemic IgA production is intact. The problem is localized to the gut lumen, suggesting a mechanism affecting IgA function or transport within the gut, not systemic production.
- (D): Prevention of IgA binding to the bacterial surface by the bacterial capsule. While capsules can interfere with antibody binding, the vignette describes an enzyme that cleaves the J chain of IgA, a mechanism distinct from preventing binding. Furthermore, the reduced IgA levels in the stool are a direct consequence of impaired sIgA formation/secretion, not necessarily impaired binding.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Mechanism (Option E)                               | Option A (Transport Impairment)                     | Option B (Fcα Cleavage)                         | Option C (Production Inhibition)                 | Option D (Capsule Interference)                 |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- |
| **Target**              | J chain of IgA                                              | IgA transport mechanism across epithelium          | Fcα region of IgA                               | IgA-producing plasma cells                      | Bacterial surface receptors for IgA             |
| **Effect**              | Prevents dimerization & secretion of sIgA                    | Reduces IgA in lumen, but mechanism is different | Degrades IgA molecule                           | Reduces serum & mucosal IgA                     | Prevents opsonization/neutralization           |
| **Pathogen Example**    | *Vibrio cholerae*, *Neisseria gonorrhoeae*                 | Less specific, can occur with epithelial damage   | Less common target for bacterial proteases      | Systemic immunodeficiency (e.g., IgA deficiency) | Encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *S. pneumoniae*) |
| **Vignette Consistency** | Directly matches description of bacterial enzyme activity   | Less specific, doesn't explain J chain cleavage   | Doesn't match description of J chain cleavage   | Contradicted by normal serum IgA levels          | Doesn't explain reduced stool IgA levels          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is understanding how pathogens evade mucosal immunity by targeting specific components of the IgA system. Specifically, bacterial IgA proteases cleave the J chain, preventing the formation and secretion of dimeric sIgA, which is crucial for neutralizing pathogens in the gut lumen. Differentiate this mechanism from impaired transport, degradation of the Fc region, inhibition of production, or interference with binding by capsules. Remember *Vibrio cholerae* and *Neisseria* species as classic examples.

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: The Immune System: Part 3 Focus 44 (innate)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement components, specifically C3 convertases (classical, alternative, lectin pathways) and their regulation by inhibitors like Factor H, in preventing excessive complement activation and subsequent tissue damage, especially in the context of immune complex deposition. (理解补体成分，特别是C3转化酶（经典、替代、凝集素途径）及其调节因子（如Factor H）在防止过度补体激活和随之而来的组织损伤中的作用，尤其是在免疫复合物沉积的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) presents to the emergency department with worsening lower extremity edema, proteinuria, and hypertension. He has been non-adherent to his prescribed hydroxychloroquine and prednisone regimen for the past 6 months. Urinalysis reveals significant hematuria and proteinuria (4+). Renal biopsy shows diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis with subepithelial immune complex deposits ("wire loop" appearance on electron microscopy) and evidence of complement deposition (C3 and C1q). Immunofluorescence microscopy confirms the presence of IgG and C3 in the glomerular deposits. Laboratory tests show low serum C3 and C4 levels, elevated anti-dsDNA antibodies, and a positive ANA titer. Further investigation reveals a mutation in the *CFH* gene, leading to decreased levels of functional Factor H protein. Which of the following mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of this patient's renal disease?

(A) Impaired formation of the C3 convertase of the alternative pathway due to lack of properdin stabilization.
(B) Defective opsonization of immune complexes by C3b, leading to reduced phagocytosis by glomerular mesangial cells.
(C) Uncontrolled activation of the C3 convertase of the alternative pathway on the surface of glomerular endothelial cells due to insufficient regulation by Factor H.
(D) Inefficient formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) due to deficiency in C5 convertase formation.
(E) Reduced production of C1q by podocytes, impairing the initiation of the classical pathway activation by immune complexes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with SLE, non-adherent to treatment. This suggests a chronic autoimmune disease prone to flares and complications.
- **Symptoms:** Lower extremity edema, proteinuria, hypertension. These are classic signs of nephrotic syndrome, often associated with glomerular damage.
- **Urinalysis:** Hematuria and proteinuria (4+). Confirms significant glomerular injury and protein leakage.
- **Renal Biopsy:** Diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis, subepithelial immune complex deposits ("wire loop"), C3/C1q deposition. This pattern is highly characteristic of lupus nephritis, specifically Class IV (diffuse proliferative). The "wire loop" appearance is due to immune complex deposition in the subendothelial space, often seen in lupus nephritis. Complement deposition indicates complement activation is involved in the pathogenesis.
- **Immunofluorescence:** IgG and C3 in glomerular deposits. Confirms immune complex deposition and complement activation.
- **Labs:** Low C3/C4, elevated anti-dsDNA, positive ANA. Consistent with an active SLE flare involving complement consumption.
- **Genetic Finding:** *CFH* gene mutation, decreased Factor H. This is the crucial clue pointing towards dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway. Factor H is a key regulator of the alternative pathway, preventing its excessive activation on host cells.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the pathogenesis. Given the SLE, immune complex deposition, complement activation, and Factor H deficiency, the question focuses on how this deficiency contributes to the glomerular damage.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Lupus Nephritis Pathogenesis:** SLE involves autoantibodies (like anti-dsDNA) forming immune complexes. These complexes deposit in tissues like the glomeruli.
- **Complement Activation:** Immune complex deposition triggers complement activation, primarily via the classical pathway (C1q binds to Fc portion of IgG) and the alternative pathway (C3b deposition on surfaces).
- **C3 Convertases:** Both pathways converge on the formation of C3 convertases (C4b2a for classical/lectin, C3bBb for alternative). These enzymes cleave C3 into C3a (anaphylatoxin) and C3b (opsonin, component of C5 convertase).
- **C5 Convertases:** C3b binds to the C3 convertase to form C5 convertases (C4b2a3b for classical/lectin, C3bBbC3b for alternative). These cleave C5 into C5a (anaphylatoxin, chemoattractant) and C5b.
- **MAC Formation:** C5b initiates the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9), which can lyse cells.
- **Role of Factor H:** Factor H is a soluble regulator of the alternative pathway. It binds to C3b and C3bBb (the alternative C3 convertase), accelerating its dissociation and acting as a cofactor for Factor I-mediated cleavage of C3b into inactive iC3b. It also competes with Bb for binding to C3b. Factor H deficiency leads to uncontrolled alternative pathway activation on host cell surfaces.
- **Glomerular Damage:** In SLE nephritis, immune complexes deposit on glomerular endothelial cells and mesangial cells. Factor H deficiency prevents proper regulation of the alternative pathway activation on these surfaces. This leads to excessive C3b deposition, formation of C3 convertases, and subsequent cleavage of C3 and C5, generating inflammatory mediators (C3a, C5a) and potentially leading to MAC formation and cell damage. This contributes significantly to the inflammation and tissue injury seen in lupus nephritis.
- **Classical Pathway:** While the classical pathway is initiated by immune complexes, the alternative pathway amplifies complement activation and contributes significantly to inflammation and tissue damage, especially when regulation is impaired.

##### 3. Linking Factor H Deficiency to the Clinical Picture:
- The patient has SLE, leading to immune complex formation and deposition in the glomeruli.
- The *CFH* mutation causes Factor H deficiency.
- Factor H normally regulates the alternative pathway on host cell surfaces (like glomerular cells).
- Deficiency of Factor H leads to uncontrolled alternative pathway activation on these cells.
- This uncontrolled activation results in excessive complement cleavage (C3, C5), inflammation (C3a, C5a), and potential cell damage (MAC), exacerbating the glomerular injury caused by the immune complexes.
- Low C3/C4 levels reflect complement consumption due to excessive activation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired formation of the C3 convertase of the alternative pathway due to lack of properdin stabilization. Properdin (Factor P) stabilizes the alternative C3 convertase (C3bBb). Deficiency of properdin leads to impaired *formation* of the alternative C3 convertase, resulting in *decreased* complement activation and increased susceptibility to certain bacterial infections (e.g., *Neisseria*). This patient has *uncontrolled* activation due to Factor H deficiency, not impaired formation.
- (B): Defective opsonization of immune complexes by C3b, leading to reduced phagocytosis by glomerular mesangial cells. C3b is a crucial opsonin. While complement activation is occurring, the primary problem in Factor H deficiency is *excessive* activation and inflammation, not defective opsonization. In fact, C3b deposition is increased due to lack of regulation. Reduced phagocytosis is not the main mechanism of damage here; rather, it's the inflammatory mediators and potential MAC-mediated damage.
- (D): Inefficient formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) due to deficiency in C5 convertase formation. Factor H deficiency leads to *increased* C3 and C5 cleavage, resulting in *more* C5 convertase formation and potentially *more* MAC formation, not less. Deficiencies in terminal complement components (C5-C9) would impair MAC formation.
- (E): Reduced production of C1q by podocytes, impairing the initiation of the classical pathway activation by immune complexes. C1q is produced primarily by the liver, not podocytes. Deficiency of C1q (or C1r/C1s) impairs the *initiation* of the classical pathway. This patient has immune complex deposition triggering both classical and alternative pathways, and the key defect is in the *regulation* of the alternative pathway, not its initiation or the classical pathway initiation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Factor H Deficiency (Correct Answer)                                  | Properdin Deficiency (Distractor A)                               | C1q Deficiency (Distractor E)                                     | Terminal Complement Deficiency (Related to Distractor D)          |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Pathway Affected** | Alternative Pathway (Regulation)                                      | Alternative Pathway (Formation of C3 Convertase)                  | Classical Pathway (Initiation)                                   | Terminal Pathway (MAC Formation)                                  |
| **Complement Levels**| Low C3, C4 (due to consumption)                                       | Normal or High C3, C4                                            | Normal C3, C4 (Classical pathway initiation impaired)            | Low C5, C6, C7, C8, C9 (Terminal components deficient)            |
| **Clinical Manifestation**| Atypical HUS, C3 Glomerulopathy, SLE exacerbation (like this case) | Increased susceptibility to *Neisseria* infections                | Increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, autoimmune disease | Increased susceptibility to *Neisseria* infections                |
| **Mechanism**        | Uncontrolled C3 convertase activity on host cells                      | Impaired C3 convertase stabilization                             | Impaired immune complex clearance, reduced inflammation           | Impaired bacterial lysis                                          |
| **Key Regulator**    | Factor H (inhibits C3bBb, cofactor for Factor I)                      | Properdin (stabilizes C3bBb)                                     | C1q (initiates classical pathway)                                 | C5b-9 components (form MAC)                                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Factor H deficiency leads to uncontrolled alternative pathway activation, causing inflammation and tissue damage, particularly in the kidney (C3 glomerulopathy, atypical HUS) and exacerbating autoimmune diseases like SLE nephritis. Remember that Factor H regulates the alternative pathway, while properdin stabilizes it. Deficiencies lead to opposite effects on complement activation.

<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Graft Rejection & Immune Cell Phenotyping
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of cellular immunology, specifically the identification of immune cell types involved in acute cellular graft rejection and their characteristic surface markers. (考察细胞免疫学，特别是参与急性细胞型移植物排斥反应的免疫细胞类型及其特征性表面标志物。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with end-stage renal disease due to diabetic nephropathy receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor with an unknown HLA type. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three weeks post-transplant, he develops fever, flank pain, elevated serum creatinine (from 1.2 mg/dL to 4.5 mg/dL), and tender, erythematous skin lesions on his lower extremities. A kidney biopsy reveals interstitial edema, tubular dilation, and infiltration of mononuclear cells. Immunofluorescence staining of the biopsy tissue shows deposition of C4d in peritubular capillaries. Flow cytometry analysis of the infiltrating mononuclear cells shows a population of cells that are CD3+, CD8+, CD4-, CD25+, and HLA-DR+. The patient's serum creatinine continues to rise despite adjustments to his immunosuppressive regimen.

Which of the following cell types is MOST likely responsible for the acute cellular rejection observed in this patient?

(A) CD4+ T helper cells
(B) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs)
(C) Natural killer (NK) cells
(D) B lymphocytes
(E) Macrophages

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, end-stage renal disease, kidney transplant recipient. This sets the stage for potential graft rejection.
- **Timeline:** 3 weeks post-transplant. This timeframe is typical for acute rejection, particularly acute cellular rejection.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, flank pain, elevated creatinine (graft dysfunction), tender erythematous skin lesions (suggestive of systemic inflammation or vasculitis, sometimes seen in rejection).
- **Biopsy Findings:** Interstitial edema, tubular dilation, mononuclear cell infiltration. These are classic histological features of acute cellular rejection.
- **Immunofluorescence:** C4d deposition in peritubular capillaries. This indicates antibody-mediated rejection (AMR) or mixed rejection, as C4d is a marker of complement activation, often associated with donor-specific antibodies (DSAs). However, the question specifically asks about the *cellular* component.
- **Flow Cytometry:** CD3+, CD8+, CD4-, CD25+, HLA-DR+. This is the crucial piece of information.
    - CD3+ indicates T cells.
    - CD8+ identifies cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
    - CD4- confirms they are not helper T cells.
    - CD25+ (IL-2 receptor alpha chain) is often expressed on activated T cells, including CTLs.
    - HLA-DR+ indicates MHC class II expression, which is typically found on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) but can also be upregulated on activated T cells.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the cell type responsible for the *acute cellular rejection*. The flow cytometry data directly points to a specific T cell subset.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection:** Primarily mediated by T lymphocytes recognizing donor MHC molecules (alloantigens) on the graft cells.
- **T Cell Subsets:**
    - **CD4+ T helper cells:** Recognize MHC class II molecules. They orchestrate immune responses by releasing cytokines, activating macrophages, and helping B cells produce antibodies. While involved in rejection, they are not the primary effector cells directly killing graft cells in acute *cellular* rejection.
    - **CD8+ Cytotoxic T Lymphocytes (CTLs):** Recognize MHC class I molecules on graft cells. Upon activation by donor alloantigens presented on MHC class I, they directly kill graft cells via perforin/granzyme release or Fas/FasL interaction. They are the main effector cells in acute cellular rejection.
    - **Natural Killer (NK) cells:** Part of the innate immune system. They can kill target cells lacking MHC class I expression or expressing stress ligands. They play a role in some types of rejection, especially in the absence of MHC matching or in certain contexts, but are not the primary mediators of classical acute cellular rejection driven by specific T cell recognition.
    - **B lymphocytes:** Produce antibodies. They are the primary mediators of antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), characterized by DSA binding to donor endothelial cells and complement activation (as suggested by C4d deposition). They are not the main effector cells in acute *cellular* rejection.
    - **Macrophages:** Phagocytic cells involved in antigen presentation and inflammation. They contribute to tissue damage in rejection but are not the primary cytotoxic effector cells.
- **Flow Cytometry Interpretation:** The described cells (CD3+, CD8+, CD4-, CD25+, HLA-DR+) are characteristic of activated CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). These cells are the key players in directly killing graft parenchymal cells (tubular cells in this case) during acute cellular rejection. The presence of C4d suggests a possible mixed rejection (cellular + antibody-mediated) or AMR, but the question specifically asks about the *cellular* component identified by flow cytometry.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **CD4+ T helper cells:** While CD4+ T cells are crucial for initiating and coordinating immune responses, including rejection (by helping CTLs and B cells), they do not directly kill target cells in the same way CTLs do. The flow cytometry data specifically identifies CD8+ cells, not CD4+ cells. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (C) **Natural killer (NK) cells:** NK cells are typically CD3- and express CD16 and CD56. They are part of the innate immune system and kill cells lacking MHC class I or expressing stress ligands. While they can contribute to rejection, they are not the primary specific effectors in acute cellular rejection driven by alloantigen recognition by T cells. The flow cytometry data shows CD3+ cells, ruling out NK cells. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (D) **B lymphocytes:** B cells are responsible for producing antibodies, which mediate antibody-mediated rejection (AMR). The C4d deposition suggests AMR might be present, but the question asks about the *cellular* rejection component identified by flow cytometry. B cells are typically CD19+ or CD20+ and do not express CD3 or CD8. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (E) **Macrophages:** Macrophages are phagocytes and antigen-presenting cells. They contribute to inflammation and tissue damage in rejection but are not the primary cytotoxic effector cells responsible for directly killing graft cells via mechanisms like CTLs. They are typically CD14+ or CD68+ and do not express CD3 or CD8. Therefore, this option is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | CD8+ CTLs (Correct Answer) | CD4+ T Helper Cells | NK Cells        | B Lymphocytes   | Macrophages     |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|----------------------|-----------------|-----------------|-----------------|
| **Primary Role in Rejection** | Direct killing of graft cells | Orchestration, help | Innate killing  | Antibody-mediated | Inflammation, APC |
| **MHC Restriction**   | MHC Class I                 | MHC Class II         | Non-MHC restricted | N/A             | N/A             |
| **Key Surface Markers** | CD3+, CD8+, TCR             | CD3+, CD4+, TCR      | CD3-, CD16+, CD56+ | CD19+, CD20+, BCR | CD14+, CD68+    |
| **Flow Cytometry Profile** | CD3+, CD8+, CD4-           | CD3+, CD4+, CD8-    | CD3-            | CD3-, CD19+     | CD3-            |
| **Mechanism of Killing** | Perforin/Granzyme, FasL     | Cytokines           | Perforin/Granzyme | Antibody production | Phagocytosis    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute cellular rejection is primarily mediated by CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) recognizing donor MHC class I molecules on graft cells. Flow cytometry is crucial for identifying specific immune cell populations based on their surface markers. Differentiating between cellular (T cell-mediated) and antibody-mediated (B cell-mediated) rejection is key for diagnosis and management. Key differentials include acute antibody-mediated rejection (AMR), chronic rejection, and infection.
